Vignéras orbifolds: isospectrality, regulators, and torsion homology
Alex Bartel and Aurel Page

Abstract
. We develop a new approach to the isospectrality of the orbifolds constructed by Vignéras. We give fine sufficient criteria for i i -isospectrality in given degree i i and for representation equivalence. These allow us to produce very small exotic examples of isospectral orbifolds: hyperbolic 3 3 -orbifolds that are i i -isospectral for all i i but not representation equivalent, hyperbolic 3 3 -orbifolds that are 0 0 -isospectral but not 1 1 -isospectral, and others. Using the same method, we also give sufficient criteria for rationality of regulator quotients Reg i ⁡ ( Y 1 ) 2 / Reg i ⁡ ( Y 2 ) 2 \Reg_{i}(Y_{1})^{2}/\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})^{2} for Vignéras orbifolds Y 1 Y_{1} , Y 2 Y_{2} , sometimes even when they are not isospectral. Moreover, we establish a link between the primes that enter in these regulator quotients and at which torsion homology of Y 1 Y_{1} and Y 2 Y_{2} can differ, and Galois representations.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Isospectral orbifolds

This paper is devoted to the venerable question
[
34
]
of which geometric and topological
properties of a closed Riemannian manifold or orbifold are encoded in the spectrum
of the Laplace-de Rham operator
\Delta
on the space of
differential
i
-forms for various
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Given
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, two closed Riemannian orbifolds are said to be

i
-isospectral
if the multisets of eigenvalues of
\Delta
on the
spaces of differential
i
-forms of these two orbifolds coincide.
We abbreviate “
0
-isospectral” to just
isospectral
, and
“
i
-isospectral for all
i
” to
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectral
.
Sunada
[
62
]
and Vignéras
[
66
]
each proposed a general
construction for pairs of orbifolds that are
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectral.
Vignéras’s method is the focus of this paper.

We briefly sketch the setup of Vignéras in a special case. In Section
4

we describe the construction in detail and in much greater generality.
Let
F
be a number field with exactly one complex place, let
D
be a division quaternion
algebra over
F
that is ramified at all real places, and let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be unit groups of two maximal orders in
D
.
A complex embedding of
F
identifies them with two discrete subgroups of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
For the rest of the introduction we fix two such groups. They act on hyperbolic
3
-space

X=\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{C}^{\times}\U_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, and under some additional conditions the closed orbifolds

\Gamma_{1}\backslash X
and
\Gamma_{2}\backslash X
are isospectral.
A lot of literature is devoted to the exploration of such conditions, see

[
44
]
and extensive references therein, but all known conditions
actually imply the stronger relationship that the
\rL^{2}
-spaces

\rL^{2}(\Gamma_{1}\backslash\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
and
\rL^{2}(\Gamma_{2}\backslash\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C}))

are isomorphic as unitary representations of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. When that relationship holds,
we say that
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are
representation equivalent
.
It is a theorem of DeTurck and Gordon
[
20
, Theorem 1.16, Remark 1.18]

that the quotients by representation equivalent groups are in fact
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectral.
In particular, as far as we are aware, the smallest currently known pair of connected
isospectral hyperbolic
3
-orbifolds comes from representation equivalent groups, and was found by
Linowitz–Voight
[
44
]
. Their orbifolds have (necessarily equal) volume
2.83366\ldots
.
The converse, whether
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectrality of two hyperbolic
3
-orbifolds
implies representation equivalence of the corresponding groups, has until
now been an open question
[
53
]
,
[
56
, §4.1]
,
[
44
, Remark 2.6]
,

[
40
, Question 8.11]
. The converse does hold for hyperbolic

2
-orbifolds
[
22
]
and it has been conjectured to hold in dimension
3
,
too
[
22
, §12]
.

In this paper we develop much finer criteria for different kinds of isospectrality
in Vignéras’s construction. Before describing the nature of
these criteria, we state some of their applications.
The following result will be proven in Example
7.4
.

Theorem A
.

There exists a pair of closed connected orientable arithmetic hyperbolic
3
-orbifolds
with volume
0.251\ldots
that are
i
-isospectral for all
i
, but not representation equivalent,
only one of which has a cyclic isotropy group of order
10
.

This might well be the smallest
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectral pair of connected
hyperbolic
3
-orbifolds.
We make two remarks on this. Firstly, there exist only finitely many hyperbolic
3
-orbifolds
of volume less than that in Theorem
A
, see
[
1
]
.
As far as we are aware, no isospectral pair with this property was known until now.
Secondly, we will see in Section
1.5
that Sunada’s
method can never produce a smaller pair.

It is known that there exist pairs of orbifolds that are
i
-isospectral for some
i

but not all
[
29
]
. However, no such examples have been known that are
3
-dimensional
or hyperbolic
[
40
, Question 8.10]
. In
[
41
]
it is predicted that such examples should exist, but that
they must be difficult to construct. We have the following application of our criteria,
which will be proven in Example
7.6
.

Theorem B
.

There exists a pair of closed connected orientable arithmetic hyperbolic
3
-orbifolds
with volume
0.246\ldots
that are isospectral, but not
1
-isospectral.

Again, one may wonder whether this is the smallest pair of connected hyperbolic isospectral
3
-orbifolds.

Our method also shows that, without any further hypotheses, the groups

\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are always “close to” representation equivalent, in the following sense.
Let
j\in\{1,2\}
. As a representation of
\U_{2}(\mathbb{C})
the space
\rL^{2}(\Gamma_{j}\backslash\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C}))

decomposes as a direct sum
\bigoplus_{V}V^{m_{V,j}}
over the irreducible representations

V
of
\U_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, where
m_{V,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Each of the isotypical subspaces
\Omega(V,\Gamma_{j})=V^{m_{V,j}}
decomposes into a direct
sum of eigenspaces
\Omega(V,\Gamma_{j})_{\Delta=\lambda}
under the Casimir operator, which
is the appropriate generalisation of the Laplace–de Rham operator.
Weyl’s law implies that as
T\to\infty
, one has

\sum_{\lambda\leq T}\dim\Omega(V,\Gamma_{j})_{\Delta=\lambda}\sim cT^{3/2}

for some constant
c>0
. The following result, which is a special case of
Theorem
5.45
, therefore implies that the difference
in the spectra for
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
is always vanishingly small, in the limit,
even when no criteria for isospectrality apply.

Theorem C
.

For every irreducible representation
V
of
\U_{2}(\mathbb{C})
there exists a constant
c_{V}\geq 0

such that for all
T>0
one has

\sum_{\lambda\leq T}\bigl|\dim\Omega(V,\Gamma_{1})_{\Delta=\lambda}-\dim\Omega(V,\Gamma_{2})_{\Delta=\lambda}\bigr|\leq c_{V}T^{1/2}.

This result defies some expert expectations. Indeed,
Kelmer proves
[
35
, Theorem 1]
that, in the opposite direction, if the groups
\Gamma_{1}

and
\Gamma_{2}
are close to being representation equivalent, then in fact they are
representation equivalent. He shows that if for all irreducible
\U_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representations

V
the quotient between the left hand side in Theorem
C
and
T^{1/2}

tends to
0
as
T\to\infty
, then
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are representation equivalent.
He then speculates that his bound
might be far from optimal, and that perhaps this type of repulsion already happens
close to the asymptotic of Weyl’s law. Theorem
C

demonstrates that in fact Kelmer’s result is sharp, since there do exist groups

\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
as above that are not representation equivalent.

1.2.
Hierarchy of isospectralities

Suppose that
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are closed orientable Riemannian manifolds of a common dimension
d
.
If they are
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectral, then the Cheeger–Müller Theorem

[
18
,
49
,
50
]
implies that one has

(1.1)

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{d}\left(\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})}{\#H_{i}(Y_{1},\mathbb{Z})_{\tors}}\right)^{(-1)^{i}}=\prod_{i=0}^{d}\left(\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})}{\#H_{i}(Y_{2},\mathbb{Z})_{\tors}}\right)^{(-1)^{i}},

where, for a Riemannian manifold
Y
, the real number
\Reg_{i}(Y)
is the covolume of the
lattice
H_{i}(Y,\mathbb{Z})/H_{i}(Y,\mathbb{Z})_{\tors}
in the inner product space
H_{i}(Y,\mathbb{R})

[
54
, §1.6]
.

There has been a lot of recent interest in understanding regulators and torsion
homology of arithmetic manifolds – see for instance
[
6
,
7
,
12
,
14
,
54
]
.
In light of equation (
1.1
), the following vague question seems natural.

Question 1.2
.

What can be said about
\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})/\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})
if
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are
i
-isospectral manifolds?

Poincaré duality implies that for a closed orientable
d
-manifold
Y

and for all
i
, one has
\Reg_{i}(Y)=\Reg_{d-i}(Y)^{-1}
. Thus,
if
d
is even, then
equation (
1.1
) says nothing about
\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})/\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})
;
while if
d
is odd, and
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectral, equation (
1.1
) implies that one has

(1.3)

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{(d-1)/2}\left(\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})^{2}}\right)^{(-1)^{i}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}.

There is no analogue of the Cheeger–Müller formula for a single degree
i
. Nevertheless,
it seems natural to wonder: if
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are merely assumed to be
i
-isospectral
for some
i
, then does it follow that
\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})^{2}}
is a rational number?
And if it is, then what primes can enter? Is there
a connection between that and the rational number
\frac{\#H_{i}(Y_{1},\mathbb{Z})_{\tors}}{\#H_{i}(Y_{2},\mathbb{Z})_{\tors}}
?
We do not know the answers to these general questions, but we prove strong results in the case
when
Y_{j}=\Gamma_{j}\backslash X
arise from the Vignéras construction.

We think of different “kinds of isospectrality” between
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}

as sitting in a kind of approximate hierarchy:
representation equivalence
1
1

1

For hyperbolic manifolds, representation equivalence of the groups is
equivalent to so-called strong isospectrality of the manifolds
[
53
]
,
but in general it is stronger.
, which we sometimes also call
\rL^{2}
-isospectrality,

\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectrality, isospectrality, which we also
refer to as
\Omega^{0}
-isospectrality, rationality of
\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})^{2}}

for all
i
, which we refer to as
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-isospectrality, and finally, for a prime
number
p
, the conditions

\ord_{p}\left(\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})^{2}}\right)=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\ord_{p}\left(\frac{\#H_{i}(Y_{1},\mathbb{Z})_{\tors}}{\#H_{i}(Y_{2},\mathbb{Z})_{\tors}}\right)=0

for all
i
, where

\ord_{p}
denotes
p
-adic valuation, which we call
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-isospectrality. Of course
that last condition only makes sense if the regulator quotients are rational in
the first place, i.e. under the assumption of
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-isospectrality.
For each of these kinds of isospectralities, we prove sufficient criteria.
We do not state them here precisely, but we give an impressionistic sketch
of the general shape of our results.

For each kind
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
of isospectrality as above, we identify certain types
of Hecke characters such that if there are no Hecke characters of that type, then
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}

are
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
-isospectral, but we do not expect the converse to hold.
For that reason we do not call the characters an obstruction, but instead
call them
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
-shady characters
: their presence merely stops
our method from proving
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
-isospectrality.
In summary, our main results take the following shape.

Theorem D
.

At least one of the following two statements is true:

(i)

there exists a number field
L
in an a-priori finite list and a
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
-shady
character of
L
;

(ii)

the groups
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
-isospectral.

For
\rL^{2}
-,
\Omega^{\bullet}
-,
\Omega^{0}
-, and
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-isospectrality, we define the respective
shady characters and prove Theorem
D
as
Theorem
5.15
, Corollaries
5.29

and
5.31
, and Theorem
5.34
, respectively.
Our theorem for
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-isospectrality is proven as Theorem
6.10
,
subject to a widely believed
conjecture on Galois representations, Conjecture
6.5
.

The conditions of being
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
-shady are completely explicit. Presence or
absence of
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
-shady can be checked efficiently using existing algorithms
for computations of Hecke characters
[
48
]
. Moreover, we have
the following implications between existence of different shady characters:

\rL^{2}
-shady

\Omega^{\bullet}
-shady

\Omega^{0}
-shady

\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-shady

for all
p

\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-shady

for
\infty
-ly

many
p

It is worth noting, in particular, that our criteria for rationality of regulator quotients
are
weaker
than those for
i
-isospectrality, and in fact
in Example
7.8
we exhibit the following peculiar phenomenon.

Theorem E
.

There exists a pair of closed connected orientable hyperbolic
3
-orbifolds
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}

with volume 5.902…that are not isospectral, nor
1
-isospectral, and for which

\dim H_{1}(Y_{1},\mathbb{R})=\dim H_{1}(Y_{2},\mathbb{R})=1
, yet
\Reg_{1}(Y_{1})^{2}/\Reg_{1}(Y_{2})^{2}
is rational.

1.3.
Homology and regulators: theorems and musings

From a number theoretic point of view, it is natural to consider the homology of
arithmetic orbifolds together with their action of Hecke operators.
Taking this additional structure into account, we considerably strengthen our results on the
homology of the
Y_{j}
sketched in Theorem
D
.

The “right” setup for Vignéras’s construction is an adélic one, in which

Y_{1}=\Gamma_{1}\backslash X
and
Y_{2}=\Gamma_{2}\backslash X
are two connected components of one
orbifold
\mathcal{Y}
. For now we continue with a special case.
Let
F
be a number field, let
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
be its ring of integers, and let
D
be a
division quaternion algebra over
F
such that
at least one infinite place of
F
splits in
D
.
There is a ray class group
C
that parametrises left ideal classes of maximal
orders of
D
, and thereby connected components of
\mathcal{Y}
;
for simplicity of the exposition, assume that
C
has order
2
,
corresponding to a quadratic extension
L
of
F
, so that one has
\mathcal{Y}=Y_{1}\sqcup Y_{2}
.
Let
\mathbb{T}_{1}
denote the Hecke algebra generated by Hecke operators
T_{\mathfrak{a}}

for all ideals
\mathfrak{a}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
whose class in
C
is trivial.
Then
\mathbb{T}_{1}
naturally acts on each of the homology groups
H_{i}(Y_{j},\mathbb{Z})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, and for
every prime number
p
we have a decomposition of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{1}
-modules

H_{i}(Y_{j},\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{n}}H_{i}(Y_{j},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}},

where the sum ranges over maximal ideals
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
of residue
characteristic
p
.
We say that such a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{n}

corresponds to
a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-shady
character
\Psi
of
L
if for every prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
that splits in
L
,
we have
T_{\mathfrak{q}}\bmod\mathfrak{n}=\Psi(\mathfrak{Q})+\Psi(\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime})
, where
\mathfrak{q}\mathbb{Z}_{L}=\mathfrak{Q}\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}
. We prove the following result as Theorem
6.12
.

Theorem F
.

Assume Conjecture
6.5
.
Let
p
be an prime number, and let
\mathfrak{n}
be a maximal ideal
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
of residue characteristic
p
that does not correspond to
a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-shady character.
Then for every
i
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{1}
-modules

H_{i}(Y_{1},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}}\cong H_{i}(Y_{2},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}}.

We could also prove an unconditional version, at the cost of replacing the
condition on
\mathfrak{n}
with a less useful one, namely
T_{\mathfrak{q}^{2}}\equiv-\norm(\mathfrak{q})\bmod\mathfrak{n}
for all
\mathfrak{q}
inert in
L
, which can be shown to be equivalent to the
previous one under Conjecture
6.5
.

It is instructive to compare Theorem
F
with work of
Calegari and Venkatesh.
In
[
14
]
, they study the torsion homology and regulators in the more
difficult setting of Jacquet–Langlands
pairs
(Y_{1}^{\prime},Y_{2}^{\prime})
of orbifolds, which share some similarities with Vignéras
orbifolds.
The orbifolds
Y_{1}^{\prime}
and
Y_{2}^{\prime}
are not isospectral, but their spectra are closely
related. In this setting there is also a Hecke algebra
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
generated by
Hecke operators
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
for maximal ideals
\mathfrak{p}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
, and acting on
all
H_{i}(Y_{j}^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})
. We say that a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
is

Eisenstein
if there exist Hecke characters
\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}
of
F
such
that
T_{\mathfrak{p}}\bmod\mathfrak{m}=\Psi(\mathfrak{p})+\Psi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p})
for all but finitely
many
\mathfrak{p}
.
Calegari and Venkatesh define a certain quotient
H_{i}(Y_{j}^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})^{\new}

of
H_{i}(Y_{j}^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})
and formulate the following
conjecture
[
14
, Conjecture 2.2.8]
.

Conjecture 1.4

(Calegari–Venkatesh)
.

Let
(Y_{1}^{\prime},Y_{2}^{\prime})
be a Jacquet–Langlands pair of
3
-orbifolds.
Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a non-Eisenstein maximal ideal of
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
.
Then we have

|H_{1}(Y_{1}^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\new}|=|H_{1}(Y_{2}^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\new}|.

In addition, they prove several partial results
[
14
, Section
6.8]
towards their conjecture, but only when averaging over all
maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}
, hence forgetting the Hecke action.
The Calegari–Venkatesh conjecture is an analogue for torsion homology of a
famous theorem from the theory of automorphic representations, the
Jacquet–Langlands correspondence (see Theorem
5.8
).
We now explain how Theorem
F
can be seen as a torsion
analogue of another automorphic phenomenon, studied by Labesse and
Langlands
[
36
]
. The relationship between
Vignéras’s construction of isospectral orbifolds and the work of
Labesse–Langlands was first exposed by Rajan
[
55
]
.
The Langlands programme postulates the existence of a compact group
L_{F}
and
conjectures that, to each
cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi
of
D^{\times}
, one should be
able to attach a
continuous irreducible representation, called a Langlands parameter,

\varphi=\varphi_{\Pi}\colon L_{F}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C}),

such that
\varphi_{\Pi}\cong\varphi_{\Pi^{\prime}}
if and only
if
\Pi\cong\Pi^{\prime}
. Moreover,
each such
\Pi
appears with multiplicity
1
in
the space of automorphic forms (see Theorem
5.6
).
In contrast, to each cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi

of the kernel
D^{1}
of the reduced norm
D^{\times}\to F^{\times}
,
one should be able to attach an irreducible Langlands parameter

\varphi\colon L_{F}\to\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C}),

but several
non-isomorphic
\Pi
can have isomorphic parameters
\varphi
: they are said
to form an
L
-packet. While the existence of the group
L_{F}
and the
parameters
\varphi
are still conjectural, Labesse and Langlands gave an ad-hoc
definition of
L
-packets for
D^{1}
, realised that sometimes not
all admissible
\Pi
in the same
L
-packet are automorphic, and proved a formula for
the multiplicity of each
\Pi
in the space of automorphic forms.
This manifests concretely as follows. To every
\Pi=\Pi_{\infty}\otimes\Pi_{f}
corresponds a Casimir eigenvalue
(coming from
\Pi_{\infty}
) and system of eigenvalues for the Hecke
operators of
D^{1}
(coming from
\Pi_{f}
), and these eigenvalues are the
same for every
\Pi
in an
L
-packet. However,
given a compact open subgroup
K_{f}
of the points of
D^{1}
over
the finite adeles, the dimension of the space
\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}

of fixed points under
K_{f}
may depend on
\Pi
, even when

\Pi
varies inside a fixed
L
-packet.
This may result in the system of eigenvalues attached to a given
L
-packet
appearing with different multiplicities in the
\rL^{2}
-space attached to
\Gamma
for the various arithmetic
groups
\Gamma=K_{f}\cap D^{1}
.
If the distributions of the automorphic multiplicities in
L
-packets were
completely random, Vignéras pairs of orbifolds would never be isospectral.
However, as Labesse and Langlands proved
[
36
, Proposition
7.2]
(see also
[
55
, Theorem 4]
), this is far from true.

Theorem 1.5

(Labesse–Langlands)
.

Suppose
\varphi\colon L_{F}\to\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is not induced from a character of
an index
2
subgroup. Then the automorphic multiplicity of every
\Pi
in the

L
-packet of
\varphi
is the same.

This strong restriction allowed Rajan to prove
[
55
, Theorem 2]
the
representation equivalence of many orbifolds generalising Vignéras’s construction.
Returning to torsion homology, let
p>2
be a prime number, and let
G_{F}
be the
absolute Galois group of
F
.
For every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
with residue field
\mathbb{F}

of characteristic
p
, there should conjecturally exist a continuous semisimple Galois
representation

\rho\colon G_{F}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})

such that one has
\Tr\rho(\Frob_{\mathfrak{q}})\equiv T_{\mathfrak{q}}\bmod\mathfrak{n}
for almost all
\mathfrak{q}
whose class in
C
is trivial, see Conjecture

6.5
.
In fact, the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{n}
does not completely determine
\rho
but it
completely determines its projectivisation

P\rho\colon G_{F}\to\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{F}).

One may think of
P\rho
as the “Langlands parameter” of the “
L
-packet”
corresponding to
\mathfrak{n}
. In addition, the condition that
\mathfrak{n}

correspond to a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-shady character
\Psi
of
L
is equivalent
to
\rho\cong\Ind_{G_{F}/G_{L}}\psi
, where
\psi
corresponds
to
\Psi
via class field theory. This makes
Theorem
F
a torsion analogue of
Theorem
1.5
.
More generally, Labesse and Langlands prove a formula for the automorphic
multiplicity of every
\Pi
.
We think that this naturally leads to a very interesting question.

Question 1.6
.

Can one formulate a torsion analogue of the full Labesse–Langlands
multiplicity formula? Can one prove it?

We also obtain a Hecke-equivariant refinement of our results on the
p
-adic
valuation of regulator ratios. It may seem difficult to make sense of it, as
regulators are only numbers and therefore cannot afford an action of the Hecke
algebra. One might hope to have a factorisation of the form
\Reg_{i}(Y_{j})=\prod_{\mathfrak{n}}\Reg_{i}(Y_{j})_{\mathfrak{n}}
, but this is unlikely to be possible, as regulators
come from constructions over
\mathbb{R}
, while decompositions according to maximal
ideals of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
are
p
-adic in nature.
However, we show that such a decomposition exists for the ratios of regulators,
under conditions ensuring that they are rational numbers.
More precisely, to certain modules
M
we attach a number
\mathcal{C}(M)
that we call
a
regulator constant
by analogy with
[
21
]
, satisfying

\mathcal{C}(H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z}))\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times},

and for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
of residue characteristic
p
,

\mathcal{C}(H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}})\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}/(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2}.

We will provide more details on our construction of regulator constants in
Section
1.6
. The following theorem is a combination of
Lemma
4.13
, Theorem
5.34
, and Theorem
6.12
.

Theorem G
.

Assume that there is no
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-shady character of
L
. Then
the following assertions hold.

(1)

For
all
i
we have

\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})^{2}}=\mathcal{C}(H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})).

(2)

Let
p
be a prime number. Then for all
i
we have

\mathcal{C}(H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z}))\equiv\prod_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathcal{C}(H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}})\bmod(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2},

where the product ranges over maximal ideals
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
of residue
characteristic
p
.

(3)

Let
p>2
be a prime number. Assume, in addition, Conjecture
6.5
.
Let
\mathfrak{n}
be a maximal ideal
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
of residue characteristic
p
that does not correspond to
a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-shady character.
Then for all
i
we have

\mathcal{C}(H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}.

The definition of
\mathcal{C}(H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}})
involves the choice of arbitrary non-degenerate

\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-valued pairings on the free parts of
H_{i}(Y_{j},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}}
satisfying certain properties.
We speculate that there should exist a canonical construction of such pairings,
leading to a notion of
p
-adic regulators
\Reg_{i,p}(Y_{j})\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}/(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2}
,
compatible with localisation at maximal ideals of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
and such that one has

\frac{\Reg_{i,p}(Y_{1})^{2}}{\Reg_{i,p}(Y_{2})^{2}}\equiv\mathcal{C}(H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z}))\bmod(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2}.

Question 1.7
.

Can one define a notion of
p
-adic regulator in this context? Of
p
-adic analytic torsion?
Is there a
p
-adic Cheeger–Müller formula?

Finally, note that the Cheeger–Müller formula merely provides a motivation for our results
on torsion homology and regulators, we do not use this formula in our proofs.
In particular our methods are very different from those of
[
14
]
.
What makes the Vignéras case more accessible than the Jacquet–Langlands case
is that the orbifolds
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are commensurable, unlike Jacquet–Langlands pairs.
The next section explains how we exploit this commensurability.

1.4.
Our approach to isospectrality

The basic idea of our approach is applicable much more widely than
in the setting sketched in the previous subsections. In Section
4
,
where we explain the general setup, we take
D
to be any division
algebra over an arbitrary number field
F
. An adélic construction then yields
a closed orbifold
\mathcal{Y}
, and the only restriction on
D
is that
\mathcal{Y}
be positive-dimensional.
One has a Hecke algebra
\mathbb{T}

acting on, say, the space
\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})
of differential
i
-forms on
\mathcal{Y}
,
and commuting with the action of the Laplace–de Rham operator
\Delta
.
That space decomposes as

\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})=\bigoplus_{c\in C}\Omega^{i}(Y_{c})

with the direct summands corresponding to the connected components
Y_{c}
of

\mathcal{Y}
, indexed by a certain class group
C
.
The Hecke algebra is generated by elements
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
indexed by maximal ideals
\mathfrak{p}

of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
. The operators
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
permute the direct summands
\Omega^{i}(Y_{c})
of
\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})
,
sending
\Omega^{i}(Y_{c})
to
\Omega^{i}(Y_{[\mathfrak{p}]c})
where
[\mathfrak{p}]
denotes the class of
\mathfrak{p}
in
C
.
In particular, the algebra
\mathbb{T}_{1}
introduced in
Subsection
1.3
is the subalgebra of
\mathbb{T}
consisting of
those Hecke operators that preserve all direct summands in the above decomposition.

Suppose once again, for simplicity, that we have
\mathcal{Y}=Y_{1}\sqcup Y_{2}
, and let

L/F
be the quadratic extension corresponding to
C
, so that
T_{\mathfrak{p}}

preserves the direct summands
\Omega^{i}(Y_{j})
if and only if
\mathfrak{p}
splits in
L/F
.
Fix
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. The algebra
\mathbb{T}
acts on the
\lambda
-eigenspace

\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})_{\Delta=\lambda}
.
If there exists a Hecke operator
T\in\mathbb{T}
inducing a map

T\colon\Omega^{i}(Y_{1})_{\Delta=\lambda}\to\Omega^{i}(Y_{2})_{\Delta=\lambda}

and acting invertibly on
\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})_{\Delta=\lambda}
, then that Hecke operator witnesses
equal multiplicity of
\lambda
in the spectra of
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
by defining an explicit
isomorphism between the respective
\Delta
-eigenspaces.
A priori one might imagine that such an invertible Hecke operator can easily fail
to exist in such a way that for every
\mathfrak{p}
that is inert in
L/F
,
there is some line in
\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})_{\Delta=\lambda}
that is annihilated by
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
. If these
different lines had nothing to do with each other, then it would be completely
unclear how to control when this happens. However, we show in Proposition

3.6
that if no Hecke operator
T\in\mathbb{T}
that sends
\Omega^{i}(Y_{1})_{\Delta=\lambda}

to
\Omega^{i}(Y_{2})_{\Delta=\lambda}
acts invertibly, then in fact there is a
single

line in
\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})_{\Delta=\lambda}
that is annihilated by
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
for every prime
\mathfrak{p}

that is inert in
L/F
. This is the first main result of Section
3
.

We deduce that if no Hecke operator that maps
\Omega^{i}(Y_{1})_{\Delta=\lambda}

to
\Omega^{i}(Y_{2})_{\Delta=\lambda}
acts invertibly,
then there is a simultaneous eigenvector in
\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})_{\Delta=\lambda}
under all the
Hecke operators such that “half” of the Hecke operators
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
have eigenvalue
a_{\mathfrak{p}}=0
.
In other words, we obtain a Hecke eigenvalue system
(a_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}}
such that for all

\mathfrak{p}
one has

a_{\mathfrak{p}}=\chi(\mathfrak{p})a_{\mathfrak{p}},

where
\chi\colon C\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is the quadratic Hecke character
attached by class field theory to the unique non-trivial character of the Galois group
\Gal(L/F)
.
This
self-twist condition
might remind the reader of coefficients
a_{p}
attached to an
elliptic curve with complex multiplication. The associated Galois representation
is then induced from a linear character of the absolute Galois group of the
quadratic extension
L
.

It is at this point in our analysis that we specialise to the case of quaternion algebras.
In this case, as the example of elliptic curves suggests,
the automorphic representation of
\GL_{2}
over
F
attached
to the Hecke eigenvalue system
(a_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}}
as above is
the automorphic induction of a Hecke character
from the quadratic extension
L/F
. In Section
5

we use the explicit classification of representations of
\GL_{2}
to arrive at precise
conditions on this Hecke character, and thus at our definitions of
\mathop{\scalebox{1.5}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
-shady
characters, and to prove various instances of Theorem
D
.

Technical discussion: algebraic axiomatisation

In Section
3
we work in a completely abstract algebraic setting:
the class group parametrising
the connected components of
\mathcal{Y}
is replaced by an arbitrary finite abelian group
C
,
the Hecke algebra
R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{T}
for a suitable ring
R
of scalars becomes a
commutative
R
-algebra
\mathbf{T}
graded by
C
, the space
that the Hecke algebra acts on, such as
\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})
or
H_{i}(\mathcal{Y})
, is just a graded
\mathbf{T}
-module.
Also in Section
3
the reader will encounter a certain group homomorphism

\nu\colon W\to C
, which could benefit from some motivating comments. Some of the components
of
\mathcal{Y}
correspond to orders in the division algebra that are conjugate to each other,
so that the components are isometric to each other. To obtain the strongest results,
we do not limit ourselves to Hecke operators that map
\Omega^{i}(Y_{1})
to
\Omega^{i}(Y_{2})
,
but consider all those mapping
\Omega^{i}(Y_{1})
to
\Omega^{i}(Y)
for any component
Y
isometric to
Y_{2}
.
Formalising this is not just a simple matter of passing to the quotient by this
equivalence relation, since such a quotient does not carry a well-defined
\mathbf{T}
-action.
The purpose of
C_{\iso}=C/\nu(W)
is to parametrise connected components up to this equivalence.
If
\widehat{C}_{\iso}
were replaced by

\widehat{C}
in all these results, then we would only pick out the elements of
\mathbf{T}

that map
\Omega^{i}(Y_{1})
to
\Omega^{i}(Y_{2})
and would get more restrictive sufficient
conditions for isospectrality.

1.5.
Comparison with the Sunada method

Sunada
[
62
]
constructs pairs of
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectral orbifolds as follows:
let
M
be a Riemannian orbifold, let
G
be a finite group acting
on
M
by isometries, and let
H
,
H^{\prime}\leq G
be two subgroups such that the linear representations

\mathbb{C}[H\backslash G]
and
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}\backslash G]
of
G
are isomorphic. Then the quotients
H\backslash M
and

H^{\prime}\backslash M
are
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectral.

The analogy between the permutation representations
\mathbb{C}[H\backslash G]
and the unitary
representations
\rL^{2}(\Gamma\backslash\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
has led many authors
to view Vignéras’s method as a special case of an extension of Sunada’s. However, as
Theorems
A
and
B
illustrate,
this framing does not do justice to the flexibility of Vignéras’s construction, which
is capable of producing isospectral orbifolds without isomorphisms of
\rL^{2}
-spaces.
Instead, in this paper we make the case that the parallel between the two methods
is of a different nature: as we explained in Section
1.4
, our conditions
ensure that the isospectralities in Vignéras’s construction are realised
by Hecke operators, which one can view as so-called
transplantation
maps.
It is well known that Sunada’s construction
can be formulated in such a way that the same is true there
[
30
, §2.2]
.

We claimed earlier that Sunada’s construction cannot yield connected hyperbolic
3
-orbifolds
of smaller volume than that appearing in Theorem
A
. We are now
in a position to explain this. The orbifolds
H\backslash M
and
H^{\prime}\backslash M
as above cover a common
orbifold, namely
G\backslash M
, with degree
[G:H]=[G:H^{\prime}]
. The smallest hyperbolic

3
-orbifold has volume
0.039\ldots

[
45
]
. Moreover, it is known that among all finite groups

G
and non-conjugate subgroups
H
,
H^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{C}[H\backslash G]
and
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}\backslash G]
are isomorphic

G
-representations, the smallest index
[G:H]
is
7

(realised by two subgroups of

G=\GL_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
)
[
52
, Theorem 3]
. Thus the smallest orbifolds that Sunada’s method could produce have
volume at least
7\cdot 0.039\ldots=0.273\ldots
.

In
[
4
]
we showed that the regulator quotients of Sunada-isospectral
manifolds
H\backslash M
,
H^{\prime}\backslash M
are rational numbers, and that for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

and all prime numbers
p\nmid\#G
we have

\ord_{p}\left(\frac{\Reg_{i}(H\backslash M)^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(H^{\prime}\backslash M)^{2}}\right)=0\text{ and }\ord_{p}\left(\frac{\#H_{i}(H\backslash M)_{\tors}}{\#H_{i}(H^{\prime}\backslash M)_{\tors}}\right)=0.

If
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
are orbifolds that come out of Vignéras’s construction,
then they typically do not sit in a common finite covering, so there is no obvious
analogue of the condition
p\nmid\#G
that would exclude all but finitely many
primes from contributing to the regulator quotients.

In Theorem
6.22
we find an analogue of “the set of prime divisors of
\#G
”
in the Vignéras setting.

Theorem H
.

Assume Conjecture
6.5
.
Then there is an explicit set
S
of prime numbers such that:

•

every prime number
p
for which there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-shady character is contained in
S
, and

•

the set
S
is infinite if and only if there exists a
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-shady character.

1.6.
Our approach to rationality of regulator quotients

As we explained in Section
1.4
, we obtain conditions under
which the spaces
H_{i}(Y_{1},\mathbb{R})
and
H_{i}(Y_{2},\mathbb{R})
are not merely abstractly isomorphic,
but such that there are isomorphisms given by Hecke operators. In particular,
they preserve the rational structure. That by itself is still not enough to show
that
\Reg_{i}(Y_{1})^{2}/\Reg_{i}(Y_{2})^{2}
is rational. The crucial additional property
is that the Hecke algebra is stable under adjoints
with respect to the harmonic forms pairing, which is used to define the regulators.
This is sufficient to imply rationality of regulator quotients and allows us to
analyse them.

In order to show this, we once again axiomatise the situation purely algebraically.
In Section
2
we first describe the very general setup.
As a by-product, we reprove in a Hecke-algebraic way a foundational result of
the Dokchitsers on so-called regulator constants,
Theorem
2.5
, which we used in
[
4
,
5
]
to analyse the
regulator quotients of Sunada-isospectral manifolds.

Then in Section
3.2
we prove that the existence of a suitable
pairing on a graded module
M
with the adjointness property described above is equivalent
to the existence of such a pairing that takes values in
\mathbb{Q}
.
The crucial ingredient in this proof is a nice criterion, Proposition
3.13
,
for existence of pairings with the required properties.
The following is a special case. We refer to Section
3
for the
definitions of the standard algebraic terms used here.

Proposition I
.

Let
C
be a finite abelian group, let
\mathbf{A}=\bigoplus_{c\in C}\mathbf{A}_{c}
be a

C
-graded commutative reduced finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, and
let
M=\bigoplus_{c\in C}M_{c}
be a finitely generated graded
\mathbf{A}
-module.
Let
L
be a field of characteristic
0
.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

there exists a non-degenerate
L
-valued bilinear pairing on
M
with respect to
which all
T\in\mathbf{A}
are self-adjoint and such that for all
c\neq c^{\prime}\in C

the homogeneous components
M_{c}
and
M_{c^{\prime}}
are orthogonal to each other;

•

for every
c\in C
the
(L\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1})
-module

L\otimes M_{c}
is self-dual.

The descent of pairings from
\mathbb{C}
to
\mathbb{Q}
easily follows from this and the Deuring–Noether
Theorem, see Proposition
3.16
.

In Section
3.3
we define regulator quotients
\mathcal{C}(M)
in this
abstract setting, a priori with respect to a given pairing,
but then we use the formalism of Section
2
to show that
in fact the values of such regulator quotients do not depend on the pairing.
In conjunction with the previously mentioned existence of
\mathbb{Q}
-valued pairings
this proves rationality of regulator quotients of Vignéras pairs.
Moreover, this formalism is very flexible, and allows us to prove Theorem
G
.

As a by-product of our approach, we have stumbled upon a curious connection between
fields generated by Hecke eigenvalues and regulator quotients. We do not pursue this
connection seriously in this paper, but Proposition
3.24
is an
example of this phenomenon. The following is a special case.

Proposition J
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{Y}
has exactly two connected components,
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
,
suppose that
\dim H_{1}(Y_{1},\mathbb{C})=\dim H_{1}(Y_{2},\mathbb{C})=1
, and suppose that there
is no
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-shady character. Then the field generated by the Hecke eigenvalues of

\mathbb{T}
acting on
H_{1}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{C})
is
\mathbb{Q}(\Reg_{1}(Y_{1})/\Reg_{1}(Y_{2}))
.

It was the observation of this numerical coincidence that suggested to us that
it should be possible to expand the Dokchitsers’ formalism of regulator constants
to Hecke algebras, and led us to our Hecke operator approach to isospectrality.

1.7.
Comparison with other approaches to Vignéras pairs

In most of the existing work devoted to Vignéras’s construction
[
66
]
, the
isospectrality is derived from the trace formula via so-called

selectivity
conditions
[
44
,
43
]
, from which one
determines which
\Gamma_{i}
intersect a given conjugacy class.
The trace formula suggests a kind of duality between this approach and ours: in the
selectivity method one studies the geometric side, while we study the spectral side.
In Corollary
5.21
, we recover a sufficient criterion for
representation equivalence that had been obtained from the selectivity method.
We believe that one might be able to obtain results on finer notions of
isospectrality, e.g.
\Omega^{\bullet}
- and
\Omega^{0}
-isospectrality, from the
selectivity method and a delicate analysis of the trace formula. It would be
interesting to work out such criteria explicitly. With our approach, working out
conditions for different notions of isospectrality amounts to different but
routine local computations. The input from the trace formula required for our
method is packaged in Langlands’s automorphic induction theorem.

An alternative approach, due to Rajan
[
55
]
, is to use the
Labesse–Langlands multiplicity formula. In Corollary
5.23
, we
recover a variant of a sufficient criterion for representation equivalence that
had been obtained by Rajan. Although the Labesse–Langlands work has so far only
been applied to representation equivalence, it is likely that one could recover
the results of Section
5
on finer notions of isospectrality
with this approach.

Our approach has two advantages:
firstly, it allows us to realise the isospectralities through direct identification
of eigenfunctions rather than just a numerical comparison of multiplicities;
and secondly and most importantly for our purposes, it applies to the torsion setting
thanks to the Hecke action on
H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

Finally, we expect that one might be able to extract some information on the algebraicity
or even rationality of the regulator quotients by relating them to special values of
L
-functions.
For an analogous observation in the Jacquet–Langlands setting see
[
14
, §6.5]
.
However, it seems hard to obtain precise information on the primes that enter
or Hecke equivariance properties along the lines of Theorem
G
.

1.8.
Notation and conventions

All our modules will be left modules. A module that is a direct sum of a number
of copies of a simple module is called
isotypical
.

If
B
is a ring,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and
M
is a
B
-module, then
\MaxSpec(B)

denotes the set of maximal ideals of
B
, the ring of
n\times n
matrices over
B

is denoted by
\Mat_{n}(B)
, and
\Ann_{B}(M)
denotes the annihilator of
M
in
B
.
If
Q
is a field and
A
is a
Q
-algebra, then
\norm_{A/Q}
denotes
the
Q
-algebra norm.
The sign
\otimes
denotes tensor product over
\mathbb{Z}
.
If
R
is a ring,
M
is an
R
-module, and
L
an
R
-algebra, then we abbreviate the

L
-module
L\otimes_{R}M
to
M_{L}
.
By an
ideal
without further specification we mean a two-sided ideal.
The
radical

\Jac(R)
of a ring
R
is defined as the
intersection of its maximal left ideals. It is a two-sided ideal.
If
p
is a prime number, then

\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}=\{a/b:a,b\in\mathbb{Z},b\not\in p\mathbb{Z}\}
denotes
the localisation of
\mathbb{Z}
at
p
, and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
denotes the ring of
p
-adic
integers.

If
C
is a finite abelian group, then
\widehat{C}=\Hom(C,\mathbb{C}^{\times})
denotes its group of complex characters.
If
X
is a set,
\sigma
is an automorphism of
X
, and
f
is a function
defined on
X
, then
f^{\sigma}
denotes the function
x\mapsto f(\sigma x)
.
We will also use this notation when
X
is a normal subgroup of a group containing
\sigma
,
in which case the automorphism of
X
is
x\mapsto\sigma x\sigma^{-1}
.
We denote the centre of a group
G
by
\centre(G)
.

When talking about homology, differential forms, etc. on orbifolds, we always
intend the meaning of these words in the orbifold sense, rather than just
in reference to the underlying topological space. We refer to
[
31
,
16
]

for the definitions and many additional details. Note that all our orbifolds
are “good” in the sense of ibid.: they have (finite) coverings by manifolds.

6.5
.

2.
Regulator constants via Hecke algebras

In this section we start by describing a very general setting in which one can
define algebraic invariants that specialise to regulator quotients in certain
number theoretic and geometric situations. We then explain how the regulator
constants of Dokchitser–Dokchitser attached to Brauer relations of finite groups

[
21
]
can be viewed as a special case of this construction, and we use
this description to give a new basis-free proof of their main algebraic theorem,

[
21
, Theorem 2.17]
. That last application will not be needed in the
remainder of the paper.

2.1.
Adjoint pairs

Throughout this section, let
R
be a commutative domain, and let
Q
be its
field of fractions. An
isogeny
of
R
-modules is a homomorphism of

R
-modules with
R
-torsion kernel and cokernel, equivalently a homomorphism

\phi
of
R
-modules such that
Q\otimes_{R}\phi
is an isomorphism of

Q
-vector spaces. Let
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
be free finite rank
R
-modules, and
let
\phi\colon N_{1}\to N_{2}
,
\phi^{*}\colon N_{2}\to N_{1}
be isogenies.
If
B_{i}
is an
R
-basis for
N_{i}
for
i=1
,
2
, then we define

\mathcal{D}_{B_{1},B_{2},\phi,\phi^{*}}=\frac{\det_{B_{1},B_{2}}\phi}{\det_{B_{2},B_{1}}\phi^{*}}\in Q^{\times},

where the determinants are computed with respect to the bases
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
.
If
B_{1}^{\prime}
and
B_{2}^{\prime}
are other
R
-bases of
N_{1}
, respectively
N_{2}
, then

\mathcal{D}_{B_{1},B_{2},\phi,\phi^{*}}\equiv\mathcal{D}_{B_{1}^{\prime},B_{2}^{\prime},\phi,\phi^{*}}\mod{(R^{\times})^{2}},

so we have a well-defined invariant

\mathcal{D}_{\phi,\phi^{*}}=\mathcal{D}_{B_{1},B_{2},\phi,\phi^{*}}(R^{\times})^{2}\in Q^{\times}/(R^{\times})^{2}

for any choice of bases
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
as above, which does not depend on these choices.
Now let
L
be a field containing
R
, and suppose that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}

are non-degenerate
L
-valued
R
-bilinear pairings on
N_{1}
, respectively
N_{2}
,
satisfying

(2.1)

\displaystyle\langle\phi n_{1},n_{2}\rangle_{2}=\langle n_{1},\phi^{*}n_{2}\rangle_{1}

for all
n_{1}\in N_{1}
and
n_{2}\in N_{2}
, in other words making the diagram

\textstyle{L\otimes_{R}N_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}}
\textstyle{L\otimes_{R}N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}}
\textstyle{\Hom(N_{1},L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{*}}
\textstyle{\Hom(N_{2},L),}

commute.
Then one has

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{B_{1},B_{2},\phi,\phi^{*}}=\frac{\det_{B_{1}}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}}{\det_{B_{2}}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}}.

This basic observation has the following two important consequences.

(O1)

The right hand side of equation (
2.2
) is independent of the
pairings, and determines a well-defined invariant in
Q^{\times}/(R^{\times})^{2}

(rather than just in
L^{\times}/(R^{\times})^{2}
), when the determinants are
evaluated with respect to any bases on
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
. More precisely, if

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}^{\prime}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}^{\prime}
are also non-degenerate
R
-bilinear pairings
on
N_{1}
, respectively
N_{2}
, with values in a field containing
R
, with respect
to which the maps
\phi
and
\phi^{*}
are adjoint as in equation (
2.1
),
then we have

\left(\frac{\det_{B_{1}}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}}{\det_{B_{2}}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}}\right)(R^{\times})^{2}=\left(\frac{\det_{B_{1}}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}^{\prime}}{\det_{B_{2}}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}^{\prime}}\right)(R^{\times})^{2}\in Q^{\times}/(R^{\times})^{2}.

(O2)

The expression
\mathcal{D}_{\phi,\phi^{*}}
is independent of the pair
\phi
,
\phi^{*}

of adjoint maps with respect to the pairings
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}
.
More precisely, if
\psi\colon N_{1}\to N_{2}
and
\psi^{*}\colon N_{2}\to N_{1}

are
R
-linear embeddings satisfying adjointness property (
2.1
)
with
\phi
replaced by
\psi
, then

\mathcal{D}_{\phi,\phi^{*}}\equiv\mathcal{D}_{\psi,\psi^{*}}\mod{(R^{\times})^{2}}
.

These consequences are particularly useful in situations in which one has
a supply of pairs of maps as above that are adjoint with respect to fixed pairings
and/or a supply of pairings that make a given pair of maps adjoint. One such
situation is provided by the formalism of Brauer relations and regulator constants.
The rest of the section is devoted to recalling that formalism, reinterpreting it
in Hecke algebra terms, and applying the above observations in that context.

2.2.
Brauer relations and regulator constants

Let
G
be a finite group. If
H
is a subgroup and
V
is an
R[G]
-module,
then we identify
\Hom_{R[G]}(R[G/H],V)
with the group
V^{H}
of
H
-fixed
points in
V
via
f\mapsto f(1\cdot H)
. In particular if
H
and
H^{\prime}

are two subgroups of
G
, then one has isomorphisms

\Hom_{R[G]}(R[G/H^{\prime}],R[G/H])\cong R[G/H]^{H^{\prime}}\cong R[H^{\prime}\backslash G/H],

where the inverses of the last and the first isomorphism are given by

(2.3)

\displaystyle H^{\prime}gH\mapsto\sum_{u\in H^{\prime}/(H^{\prime}\cap{}^{g}H)}ugH\quad\text{and}\quad gH\mapsto(\alpha\colon hH^{\prime}\mapsto hgH),

respectively. For
g\in G
, we will denote the element of
\Hom_{R[G]}(R[G/H^{\prime}],R[G/H])

corresponding to the double coset
H^{\prime}gH
by
T_{H^{\prime}gH}
.
Composition of homomorphisms defines a product on
R[H\backslash G/H]
. This

R
-algebra is called a
Hecke algebra
, and its elements are called

Hecke operators
.
There is an
R
-linear map

\displaystyle R[H^{\prime}\backslash G/H]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle R[H\backslash G/H^{\prime}],

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle T^{*},

defined by
(T_{H^{\prime}gH})^{*}=T_{Hg^{-1}H^{\prime}}
.
Moreover, for every module
V
over the group ring
R[G]
, there is a map

\displaystyle\Hom_{R[G]}(R[G/H^{\prime}],R[G/H])

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\Hom_{R}(V^{H},V^{H^{\prime}})

(2.4)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha}=(f\mapsto f\circ\alpha).

Slightly abusing notation, we will sometimes denote the image of an element
T

of
\Hom_{R[G]}(R[G/H^{\prime}],R[G/H])

under this map by
T\colon V^{H}\to V^{H^{\prime}}
.

Now let
V
be a finitely generated
R[G]
-module that is free over
R
,
and let
S_{1}=\bigsqcup_{i}G/H_{i}
and

S_{2}=\bigsqcup_{j}G/H_{j}^{\prime}
be finite
G
-sets, where
H_{i}
and
H_{j}^{\prime}
are
subgroups of
G
.
Define

V^{S_{1}}=\Hom_{R[G]}(R[S_{1}],V)\cong\bigoplus_{i}V^{H_{i}},\;\;\;V^{S_{2}}=\Hom_{R[G]}(R[S_{2}],V)\cong\bigoplus_{j}V^{H_{j}^{\prime}},

where
R[S_{i}]
are the
R
-linear permutation modules attached to
S_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then similarly to (
2.2
), an element
T\in\Hom_{R[G]}(R[S_{2}],R[S_{1}])

induces an
R
-homomorphism
T\colon V^{S_{1}}\to V^{S_{2}}
.

We say that the pair
\Theta=(S_{1},S_{2})
is a
Brauer relation
if there is
an isogeny of
R
-modules in
\Hom_{R[G]}(R[S_{2}],R[S_{1}])
.

Assume that
\Theta=(S_{1},S_{2})
is a Brauer relation.
If
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is a non-degenerate
R
-bilinear
G
-invariant pairing on
V

with values in a field
L
containing
R
,
and
U
is a subgroup of
G
, then let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{U}
be the pairing on
V^{U}

defined by
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{U}=\frac{1}{\#U}\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle

for
v_{1}
,
v_{2}\in V^{U}
. It is not hard to see that this pairing is
non-degenerate on
V^{U}
. Thus, such a pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
induces an

R
-bilinear non-degenerate pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}
on
V^{S_{1}}
whose restriction to
each direct summand
V^{H_{i}}
is given by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H_{i}}
and that makes the
distinct direct summands orthogonal, and analogously there is an induced
non-degenerate pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}
on
V^{S_{2}}
. The
regulator constant

of
V
with respect to the Brauer relation
\Theta
is defined as

\mathcal{C}_{\Theta}(V)=\frac{\det(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}|V^{S_{1}})}{\det(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}|V^{S_{2}})}\in L^{\times}/(R^{\times})^{2}.

Theorem 2.5

(
[
21
, Theorem 2.17]
)
.

The value of
\mathcal{C}_{\Theta}(V)
does not depend on
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, i.e.
if
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\prime}
are non-degenerate
G
-invariant
R
-bilinear
pairings on
V
, inducing pairings
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{j}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{j}^{\prime}
,
respectively, on
V^{S_{j}}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
as above, then

\frac{\det(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}^{\prime}|V^{S_{1}})}{\det(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}^{\prime}|V^{S_{2}})}\equiv\frac{\det(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}|V^{S_{1}})}{\det(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}|V^{S_{2}})}\mod{(R^{\times})^{2}}.

We now give a new proof of Theorem
2.5
, using the
observations of Section
2.1
.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

We claim that if
H
and
H^{\prime}
are subgroups of
G
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is a non-degenerate

G
-invariant
R
-bilinear pairing on
V
, then the adjoint of

T_{H^{\prime}gH}\colon V^{H}\to V^{H^{\prime}}

with respect to the induced pairings
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H^{\prime}}

is
T_{Hg^{-1}H^{\prime}}\colon V^{H^{\prime}}\to V^{H}
. Indeed, an elementary calculation shows that there is a
well-defined function
H^{\prime}gH\to H^{\prime}/(H^{\prime}\cap{}^{g}H)
satisfying
ugh\mapsto u(H^{\prime}\cap{}^{g}H)

for all
u\in H^{\prime}
and
h\in H
, all of whose fibres have cardinality
\#H
,
whence we deduce that for every
v\in V^{H}
and
v^{\prime}\in V^{H^{\prime}}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#{H^{\prime}}}\langle T_{{H^{\prime}}gH}v,v^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#{H^{\prime}}}\sum_{u\in{H^{\prime}}/({H^{\prime}}\cap{}^{g}H)}\langle ugv,v^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#{H^{\prime}}\cdot\#H}\sum_{x\in{H^{\prime}}gH}\langle xv,v^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#{H^{\prime}}\cdot\#H}\sum_{y\in Hg^{-1}{H^{\prime}}}\langle v,yv^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#H}\sum_{h\in H/(H\cap{}^{g^{-1}}{H^{\prime}})}\langle v,hg^{-1}v^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#H}\langle v,T_{Hg^{-1}{H^{\prime}}}v^{\prime}\rangle,

as claimed.

In particular, it follows that if
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\prime}
are
non-degenerate
G
-invariant
R
-bilinear pairings on
V
, and

T\in\Hom_{R[G]}(R[S_{2}],R[S_{1}])
is an isogeny, then the adjoint
of the induced isogeny
T\colon V^{S_{1}}\to V^{S_{2}}

with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}
is the same as with respect
to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}^{\prime}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}^{\prime}
, namely equal to
T^{*}
in both cases.
Thus,
\mathcal{C}_{\Theta}(V)=\mathcal{D}_{T,T^{*}}
,
and in particular is the same when computed with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

as with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\prime}
.
∎

3.
Algebras graded by finite abelian groups

In this section we continue working in a purely algebraic setting, investigating graded
modules over certain rings graded by a finite abelian group.

The main result of Section

3.1
is Proposition
3.6
, in which we give a sufficient criterion
for existence of an isomorphism between different homogeneous components of such a graded
module. In the geometric setting this isomorphism will realise an isospectrality.
To prove Proposition
3.6
we first construct paths of isomorphisms
for the simple subquotients, using Lemma
3.5
, and then piece them
together using dévissage, Lemma
3.3
.

In Section
3.2
we investigate certain pairings on our graded
modules, which we call polarisations.
The main result is Proposition
3.13
, a necessary and sufficient criterion
for existence of polarisations, which in particular
implies Proposition
I
from the Introduction.
An important consequence of this criterion is Proposition
3.16
,
which says that the existence of a polarisation with values in a field extension
implies the existence of one with values in the base field. This is an important
ingredient in our proof of rationality of quotients of regulators in the geometric setting.

In Section
3.3
we apply the formalism of adjoint pairs from Section
2.1

to define our notion of regulator constants and to prove in Proposition
3.19

that it is independent of any choices involved. These invariants become quotients of regulators
in the geometric setting. They enjoy some natural properties, such as rationality (Corollary
3.21
),
triviality (Lemma
3.22
), good behaviour with respect to extension of scalars
(Lemma
3.23
), and with respect to various decompositions (Lemma
3.25
,
Proposition
3.26
, and Proposition
3.27
).

The following notation and assumptions will remain in place for the rest of
the section. Let
R
be a commmutative Noetherian domain
and let
Q
be its field of fractions.
An
R
-algebra
is a ring

\mathbf{A}
equipped with a ring homomorphism from
R
to the centre of
\mathbf{A}
.
If
C
is a group and
\mathbf{A}
is an
R
-algebra, then a
grading of
\mathbf{A}

by
C
is a collection of
R
-submodules
\mathbf{A}_{c}\subset\mathbf{A}
indexed by

c\in C
such that there is a direct sum decomposition of
R
-modules

\mathbf{A}=\bigoplus_{c\in C}\mathbf{A}_{c}
and such that for all
c
,
c^{\prime}\in C
one has

\mathbf{A}_{c}\mathbf{A}_{c^{\prime}}\subset\mathbf{A}_{cc^{\prime}}
. An
R
-algebra that is equipped with a grading
by a group
C
is said to be
graded by
C
, and the
R
-submodules

\mathbf{A}_{c}
of such an
R
-algebra
\mathbf{A}
are referred to as the
homogeneous components

of
\mathbf{A}
.

For the rest of the section, let
\mathbf{T}
be a commutative
R
-algebra that
is graded by a finite abelian group
C
. The trivial homogeneous component
\mathbf{T}_{1}

is an
R
-subalgebra of
\mathbf{T}
, and for every
c\in C
, the
R
-submodule
\mathbf{T}_{c}

of
\mathbf{T}
is an
\mathbf{T}_{1}
-module. Next, let
W
be a group equipped with a group
homomorphism
\nu\colon W\to C
. By setting
W_{c}=\nu^{-1}(c)

for
c\in C
, we obtain a
C
-grading on the group
algebra
\mathbf{T}[W]
. Let
C_{\iso}=C/\nu(W)
. Thus,
\widehat{C}_{\iso}

is canonically identified with the group of all
\chi\in\widehat{C}
that are trivial on
\nu(W)
.
Let
\mathbf{A}
be a quotient of
\mathbf{T}[W]
by a homogeneous ideal, equivalently
a
C
-graded
R
-algebra together with a grading-preserving surjection from
\mathbf{T}[W]
, and
assume that
\mathbf{A}
is finitely generated as an
R
-module.
In particular, every
\mathbf{A}
-module is also a
\mathbf{T}[W]
-module.

For us, a
graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
is a finitely generated

\mathbf{A}
-module
M
equipped with a collection of
R
-submodules
M_{c}\subset M

indexed by
c\in C
such that one has a direct sum decomposition
of
\mathbf{T}_{1}
-modules
M=\bigoplus_{c\in C}M_{c}
and such that for all
c
,

c^{\prime}\in C
one has
\mathbf{A}_{c}M_{c^{\prime}}\subset M_{cc^{\prime}}
.

If an element of a ring is invertible modulo the radical, then it is invertible.
A ring is called
semilocal
if its quotient by the radical
is a semisimple ring. A commutative ring is semilocal if and only if it has
only finitely many maximal ideals. We refer to
[
37
]
for these
definitions and basic facts from ring theory.

3.1.
Linked modules

Definition 3.1
.

Let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module, and
let
c,c^{\prime}\in C
. If
L
is a ring containing
R
, we say that
M_{c}

is
L
-linked
to
M_{c^{\prime}}
by
T\in\mathbf{A}_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}}
if the
induced map
T\colon(M_{c})_{L}\to(M_{c^{\prime}})_{L}
is an isomorphism.
We say that
M_{c}
is
L
-linked
to
M_{c^{\prime}}
if it is
L
-linked to
it by some
T\in\mathbf{A}_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}}
.
We say that
M_{c}
is
linked
to
M_{c^{\prime}}
if for some ring
L

containing
R
, the module
M_{c}
is
L
-linked to
M_{c^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is a field. Let
c\in C
, and let
M
be a non-zero

\mathbf{A}
-module. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

there exists an element in
\mathbf{A}_{c}
that is invertible in
\mathbf{A}
;

(2)

there exists a simple subquotient
N
of
M
and an
element of
\mathbf{A}_{c}
that does not annihilate
N
.

If, moreover, the image of
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{A}
is reduced, then the conditions are
equivalent to the following:

(3)

the homogeneous component
\mathbf{A}_{c}
does not annihilate
M
.

Proof.

The forward implications are trivial, without the reduced assumption.

We now prove that
3
implies
2
.
The homogeneous component
\mathbf{A}_{c}
is generated by the images of

\mathbf{T}_{d}\cdot W_{d^{-1}c}
, as
d
runs over
C
. The hypothesis
therefore implies that there exist
d\in C
,
T\in\mathbf{T}_{d}
, and
w\in W_{d^{-1}c}

such that
Tw
does not annihilate
M
. Since
w
is invertible, this is equivalent
to
T
not annihilating
M
. Since the image of
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{A}
is reduced
and finite-dimensional over the field
\mathbf{T}_{1}
, it is a product of
fields, so its quotient
\mathbf{T}/\Ann_{\mathbf{T}}M
is reduced.
The element
T
is therefore not nilpotent in
\End_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}(M)
.
Since
M
has finite length,
this implies that
T
cannot annihilate every simple subquotient of
M
,
and therefore the same is true for
Tw\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
.

Finally we prove that
2
implies
1
.
By the same argument as in the previous paragraph, the assumption implies that
there exist
d\in C
,
T\in\mathbf{T}_{d}
, and
w\in W_{d^{-1}c}
such that

Tw
does not annihilate a subquotient
N
, equivalently that
T
does not annihilate
N
,
equivalently that
T
acts invertibly on
N
. Let
n
be the order of
d\in C
.
Then
T^{n}\in\mathbf{T}_{1}
also acts invertibly on
N
. Since
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is a field,
this implies that
T^{n}
is invertible, therefore so is
Tw
, whose image in
\mathbf{A}

belongs to
\mathbf{A}_{c}
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose that
R
is semilocal. Let
c\in C
,
and let
M
be an
\mathbf{A}
-module such that for every simple subquotient

N
of
M
, there exists an element of
\mathbf{A}_{c}
that acts non-trivially
(equivalently invertibly) on
N
. Then there exists an element of
\mathbf{A}_{c}

that acts invertibly on
M
.

Proof.

By replacing
\mathbf{A}
by
\mathbf{A}/(\Ann_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}M)\cdot\mathbf{A}
,
we may assume that
\mathbf{T}_{1}
acts faithfully on
M
.
Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a maximal ideal of
\mathbf{T}_{1}
. Then by faithfulness,
it is the annihilator of a simple
\mathbf{T}_{1}
-subquotient

M^{\prime}
of
M
. By Lemma
3.2
, applied to
\mathbf{T}_{1}/\mathfrak{m}

in place of
\mathbf{T}_{1}
and to
M^{\prime}
in place of
M
, there exists
u_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
that acts invertibly on

M/\mathfrak{m}M
. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, there exists
u\in\mathbf{A}_{c}

that acts invertibly on
M/\Jac(\mathbf{T}_{1})M
, where recall that

\Jac(\mathbf{T}_{1})=\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\MaxSpec(\mathbf{T}_{1})}\mathfrak{m}

denotes the radical of
\mathbf{T}_{1}
. By

[
37
, Corollary 5.9]
,
\Jac(\mathbf{T}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{A}
is contained in the radical
of
\mathbf{A}
, so
u
acts invertibly on
M
.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose that
R
is semilocal. Let
M
be a
\mathbf{T}
-module.
Then for every simple
\mathbf{T}
-subquotient
E
of
M

there exists
\mathfrak{p}\in\MaxSpec(R)
such that
E
is isomorphic to a

\mathbf{T}
-submodule of
R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M
.

Proof.

By replacing
\mathbf{T}
by
\mathbf{T}/\Ann_{\mathbf{T}}M\subset\End M
, we may assume
that
\mathbf{T}
is finitely generated over
R
.
Let
E
be a simple subquotient of
M
. Then its annihilator is a maximal
ideal
\mathfrak{P}
of
\mathbf{T}
. By
[
23
, Corollary 4.17]
the intersection

\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{P}\cap R
is a maximal ideal of
R
, and
E
is a subquotient
of the localisation
(R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M)_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Now, the quotient
R/\mathfrak{p}
is
a field, so that
\mathbf{T}/\mathfrak{p}\mathbf{T}
is Artinian. By
[
23
, Corollary 2.16]
,
the localisation
(\mathbf{T}/\mathfrak{p}\mathbf{T})_{\mathfrak{P}}
is local and is a direct factor of

\mathbf{T}/\mathfrak{p}\mathbf{T}
, so that
(R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M)_{\mathfrak{P}}
is a direct
\mathbf{T}
-summand of

R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M
. Since this localisation is Artinian, it contains
a simple submodule. But since
(\mathbf{T}/\mathfrak{p}\mathbf{T})_{\mathfrak{P}}
is local, all simple

(\mathbf{T}/\mathfrak{p}\mathbf{T})_{\mathfrak{P}}
-modules are pairwise isomorphic, and in particular

E
is isomorphic to a simple
\mathbf{T}
-submodule of

(R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M)_{\mathfrak{P}}\subset R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M
, which proves the result.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
c\in C
be non-trivial, and for every
\chi\in\widehat{C}
with
\chi(c)\neq 1
,
let
c_{\chi}\in C
be such that
\chi(c_{\chi})\neq 1
. Then

c
is contained in the group generated by the elements
c_{\chi}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
X
is the set of all
\chi\in\widehat{C}
satisfying
\chi(c)\neq 1
.
Let
U
be the subgroup of
C
generated by
c_{\chi}
for all
\chi\in X
.
If
c
is not in
U
,
then there exists
\chi^{\prime}\in\widehat{C}
such that
\chi^{\prime}(c)\neq 1

and
\chi^{\prime}|_{U}=1
, in particular such that we have
\chi^{\prime}\not\in X
. Therefore,
c
is in
U
,
as claimed.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

Suppose that
R
is semilocal. Let
c\in C
, and let

M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module. Suppose that for all the finitely many

\mathfrak{p}\in\MaxSpec(R)
, for all simple (ungraded)
\mathbf{T}
-submodules
E

of
R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M
, and for all
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}
with

\chi(c)\neq 1
, there exist
c_{\chi,E}\in C
with

\chi(c_{\chi,E})\neq 1
and
T_{\chi,E}\in\mathbf{T}_{c_{\chi,E}}
that
does not annihilate
E
. Then
there exists an element of
\mathbf{A}_{c}
that acts invertibly
on
M
.

Proof.

Let
N
be a simple
\mathbf{A}
-subquotient of
M
.
We claim that the hypothesis implies that for all
\chi\in\widehat{C}
satisfying
\chi(c)\neq 1

there exist
c_{\chi,N}\in C
with
\chi(c_{\chi,N})\neq 1
and

S_{\chi,N}\in\mathbf{A}_{c_{\chi,N}}
that does not annihilate
N
.
Indeed, for
\chi\in\widehat{C}
that do not vanish on
\nu(W)
, we may take

S_{\chi,N}
to be the image of any
w\in W
for which
\chi(\nu(w))\neq 1
.
Suppose instead that
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}
satisfies
\chi(c)\neq 1
.
Since the image of
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{A}
is central and
N
is finite over
R
,
the
\mathbf{T}
-module

N
is isomorphic to
E^{r}
for a simple
\mathbf{T}
-module
E
and some
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
By Lemma
3.4
, the module
E
is isomorphic to a simple submodule of

R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M
for some
\mathfrak{p}\in\MaxSpec(R)
. We may then take

S_{\chi,N}=T_{\chi,E}
.
By Lemma
3.5
, there exist
\alpha_{\chi,N}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

c=\prod_{\chi}c_{\chi,N}^{\alpha_{\chi,N}}
, so that

\prod_{\chi}S_{\chi,N}^{\alpha_{\chi,N}}
belongs to
\mathbf{A}_{c}
. This product
clearly acts invertibly on
N
. The conclusion follows from Lemma
3.3
.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
c\in C
and let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module.
Suppose that for all simple (ungraded)
\mathbf{T}
-submodules
E

of
M_{Q}
, and for all
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}
with

\chi(c)\neq 1
, there exist
c_{\chi,E}\in C
with

\chi(c_{\chi,E})\neq 1
and
T_{\chi,E}\in\mathbf{T}_{c_{\chi,E}}
that
does not annihilate
E
.
Then for every
b\in C
, the homogeneous component
M_{b}
is linked to
M_{cb}
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.6
with
R=Q
.
∎

3.2.
Polarisations

Definition 3.8
.

An
involution
on
\mathbf{T}
is an

R
-module homomorphism
\iota\colon\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{T}

satisfying the following properties:

•

\iota^{2}=\id
,

•

for all
x
,
y\in\mathbf{T}
one has
\iota(xy)=\iota(y)\iota(x)
,

•

for all
c\in C
one has
\iota(\mathbf{T}_{c})=\mathbf{T}_{c^{-1}}
.

In particular, every involution on
\mathbf{T}
stabilises
\mathbf{T}_{1}
.
Extend any involution
\iota
on
\mathbf{T}
to an involution
on
\mathbf{T}[W]
by defining
\iota(w)=w^{-1}
for all
w\in W
, and
assume that this involution descends to
\mathbf{A}
.
Whenever
M
is an
\mathbf{A}
-module (resp.
\mathbf{T}_{1}
-module), we give
M^{*}=\Hom_{R}(M,R)
the
structure of an
\mathbf{A}
-module (resp.
\mathbf{T}_{1}
-module), called the
dual of
M
,
via
\iota
, i.e.
(T\phi)(m)=\phi(\iota(T)m)
for
\phi\in M^{*},m\in M

and
T\in\mathbf{A}
(resp.
T\in\mathbf{T}_{1}
). We say that an
\mathbf{A}
-module
(resp.
\mathbf{T}_{1}
-module)
M
is
self-dual
if
M^{*}
is isomorphic to
M
.

Assumption 3.9
.

For the rest of the section, assume that the image of
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{A}
is reduced.
Further, assume that
\mathbf{T}
is equipped with an involution
\iota
, and
that
\iota
, when extended to
\mathbf{T}W
as above, descends to
\mathbf{A}
.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is
R
-torsion-free.
If
L
is a field containing
Q
, then a
polarisation
on
M

over
L
is a non-degenerate bilinear pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon M\otimes_{R}M\to L
such that for
c\neq c^{\prime}\in C
, the module
M_{c}
is orthogonal
to
M_{c^{\prime}}
, and such that for every
T\in\mathbf{A}
, the element
T
is adjoint to

\iota(T)
. If
M
is equipped with a polarisation over
L
, then we say
that
M
is
polarised
over
L
, and if
M
admits a polarisation
over
L
, then we say that
M
is
polarisable
over
L
.

Remark 3.11
.

A pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon M\otimes_{R}M\to L
is a polarisation
for
\mathbf{A}
if and only if it is a polarisation for
\mathbf{T}

that is
W
-invariant, i.e. such that for all
w\in W
and all
m,m^{\prime}\in M

we have
\langle wm,wm^{\prime}\rangle=\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle
.

Remark 3.12
.

If
M
is as in Definition
3.10
, then

M^{*}=\Hom_{R}(M,R)
is a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module, where for every
c\in C
,
the homogeneous component
(M^{*})_{c}
consists of the homomorphisms
M\to R
that
factor through
M_{c^{-1}}
.
A polarisation on
M
over a field
L
is the same as an isomorphism of
(not graded)
\mathbf{A}_{L}
-modules
M_{L}\to(M^{*})_{L}
that sends

M_{c}
to
(M^{*})_{c^{-1}}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is
R
-torsion-free,
and let
L
be a field containing
Q
.
Then
M
is polarisable over
L
if and only if for every
c\in C
,

(M_{c})_{L}
is a self-dual
(\mathbf{A}_{1})_{L}
-module.

Proof.

The “only if” direction is clear, so we prove the other direction.

We may, without loss of generality, replace
R
by
L
, replace all the algebras
and modules by their tensor product with
L
over
R
, and replace
\mathbf{T}

by its image in
\mathbf{A}
. Then
\mathbf{T}
, being a reduced
commutative algebra over a field by Assumption
3.9
, is a product of fields. In particular, every
quotient of
\mathbf{T}
is also reduced.
The algebra
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is a product of factors
B
that are each
either a field that is stabilised by
\iota
, or a product
B=A_{1}\times A_{2}
of fields
with
\iota
inducing an isomorphism from
A_{1}
to
A_{2}
and from
A_{2}
to
A_{1}
.
Accordingly the tensor products
B\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}\bullet
for all such factors
B

induce a direct product decomposition on the

\mathbf{T}_{1}
-algebras
\mathbf{A}_{1}
,
\mathbf{T}
, and
\mathbf{A}
, and a direct
sum decomposition on the modules
M
and
M_{c}
for all
c\in C
. Clearly,
M
is
polarisable if and only if for all
B
as above the module
B\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}M
is,
and similarly, for a given
c\in C
, the
\mathbf{A}_{1}
-module
M_{c}
is self-dual
if and only if for all
B
, the
B\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{1}
-module

B\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}M_{c}
is. Moreover,
\mathbf{T}
being reduced implies that
so is
B\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}\mathbf{T}
for all
B
as above.
Thus, we assume, without loss of generality, that

\mathbf{T}_{1}
is either a field stabilised by
\iota
or isomorphic to
A_{1}\times A_{2}

as just described.

Let

\tilde{C}=\{c\in C:\mathbf{A}_{c}M\neq 0\}.

For an arbitrary
T\in\mathbf{A}
, the non-degeneracy of the pairing on
M
implies
that
TM\neq 0
if and only
\iota(T)M\neq 0
. It follows that if
c\in C
, and

\mathbf{T}_{1}=A_{1}\times A_{2}
, then
(A_{1}\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}\mathbf{A})_{c}

does not annihilate
M
if and only if
(A_{2}\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}\mathbf{A})_{c}
does not.
Therefore, Lemma
3.2
implies that we have

\tilde{C}=\{c\in C:\mathbf{A}_{c}\text{ contains an invertible element}\}.

In particular,
\tilde{C}
is a subgroup of
C
.

Now, suppose that for every
c
, the
\mathbf{A}_{1}
-module
(M_{c})_{L}
is self-dual.
Let
D
be a complete set of coset representatives for
C/\tilde{C}
,
and for each
d\in D
, let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{d}
be a non-degenerate
L
-valued

L
-bilinear pairing on
M_{d}
such that for all
T\in\mathbf{A}_{1}
, the
operator
\iota(T)
is the adjoint of
T
. For every
c\in\tilde{C}
, fix
an invertible element
T_{c}\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
. For
c\in\tilde{C}
and
d\in D
,
define a pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{cd}
on
M_{cd}
by setting, for every
m
,
m^{\prime}\in M_{cd}
,

\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle_{cd}=\langle T_{c}^{-1}m,\iota(T_{c})m^{\prime}\rangle_{d}.

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be the unique pairing on
M
that restricts to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{c}

for all
c\in C
and that makes the different homogeneous components pairwise orthogonal.
It is clear that each
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{c}
is non-degenerate, therefore so is
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
We claim that for all
T\in\mathbf{A}
, the adjoint of
T
with respect to this pairing is

\iota(T)
. It suffices to prove this for homogeneous elements
T
. Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\tilde{C}
,

d\in D
,
T\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
,
m\in M_{c^{-1}c^{\prime}d}
and
m^{\prime}\in M_{c^{\prime}d}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\langle Tm,m^{\prime}\rangle_{c^{\prime}d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle T_{c^{\prime}}^{-1}Tm,\iota(T_{c^{\prime}})m^{\prime}\rangle_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\underbrace{(T_{c^{\prime}}^{-1}TT_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}})}_{\in\mathbf{A}_{1}}T_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}}^{-1}m,\iota(T_{c^{\prime}})m^{\prime}\rangle_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle T_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}}^{-1}m,\iota(T_{c^{\prime}}^{-1}TT_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}})\iota(T_{c^{\prime}})m^{\prime}\rangle_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle T_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}}^{-1}m,\iota(T_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}})\iota(T)m^{\prime}\rangle_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle m,\iota(T)m^{\prime}\rangle_{c^{-1}c^{\prime}d},

as required. This shows that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is a polarisation on
M
,
and completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

For
M
as in Definition
3.10
to be polarisable over a
field
L
, it is not sufficient that
M_{L}
be a self-dual
(\mathbf{A}_{1})_{L}
-module. Indeed,
let
W=1
,
\mathbf{A}_{1}=\mathbf{T}_{1}=Q\times Q
, with
\iota
swapping the two factors,

\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{T}_{1}
with
trivial grading by a group
C=\{1,c\}
of order
2
, and let
M_{1}=Q
with
\mathbf{T}
acting
via projection onto the first factor
Q
, and
M_{c}=Q
with
\mathbf{T}
acting via
projection onto the second factor. Then
M_{1}
and
M_{c}
are dual to each other,
so that
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{c}
is a self-dual
\mathbf{T}_{1}
-module. However,
M_{1}
is not
self-dual, so
M
is not polarisable over
Q
.

Corollary 3.15
.

Assume that
W=1
and that either
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is a field or
\iota
is trivial.
Let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{T}
-module that is
R
-torsion-free.
Then
M
is polarisable over
Q
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
R=Q
and replace
\mathbf{T}
by its
image in
\mathbf{A}
.
Under the assumptions,
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is a product of fields, each preserved
by
\iota
, and
M
is polarisable over
Q
if and only if it is after taking the
tensor product with each of these factors. Thus, it suffices to assume that
\mathbf{T}_{1}

is a field.
In that case, for every
c\in C
the
\mathbf{T}_{1}
-vector spaces
(M_{c})_{Q}
and
(M^{*}_{c})_{Q}
have
the same dimension, and are therefore isomorphic. The result follows from Proposition
3.13
.
∎

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is
R
-torsion-free, and
let
L
be a field containing
Q
.
Then
M
is polarisable over
Q
if and only if
M
is polarisable over
L
.

Proof.

Assume that
M
is polarisable over
L
. Let
c\in C
be arbitrary.
Then the
(\mathbf{T}_{1})_{L}
-module
(M_{c})_{L}
is
self-dual, i.e.
(M_{c})_{L}
and
(M_{c}^{*})_{L}
are
isomorphic. By the Deuring–Noether Theorem
[
37
, Theorem 19.25]

this implies that
(M_{c})_{Q}
and
(M_{c}^{*})_{Q}
are isomorphic, i.e. the
(\mathbf{T}_{1})_{Q}
-module
(M_{c})_{Q}

is self-dual. By Proposition
3.13
, the module
M
is polarisable over
Q
.
∎

Definition 3.17
.

We will say that a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
M
is
polarisable

if it is polarisable over some field containing
Q
. In light of Proposition

3.16
this is equivalent to
M
being polarisable over
Q
.

3.3.
Regulator constants for graded modules

In this subsection we continue to make Assumption
3.9
.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is free over
R
.
Let
c,c^{\prime}\in C
. Suppose that
M
is polarisable
and that
M_{c}
is
L
-linked to
M_{c^{\prime}}
by
T\in(\mathbf{A}_{c^{\prime}c^{-1}})_{L}

for some field
L
containing
Q
.
Then
\iota(T)
, being the adjoint of an isomorphism with respect to a
polarisation, induces an isomorphism
(M_{c^{\prime}})_{L}\to(M_{c})_{L}
.
Define the
regulator constant of
M
with respect to the pair
(c,c^{\prime})
by

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)=\frac{\det(T\colon(M_{c})_{L}\to(M_{c^{\prime}})_{L})}{\det(\iota(T)\colon(M_{c^{\prime}})_{L}\to(M_{c})_{L})}=\mathcal{D}_{T,\iota(T)}\in L^{\times}/(R^{\times})^{2},

using the notation of Section
2.1
, where recall that the
determinants are evaluated with respect to any bases
B_{1}
of
M_{c}
and

B_{2}
of
M_{c^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 3.19
.

For every polarisation
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
M
we have

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)=\frac{\det(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\,|\,M_{c})}{\det(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\,|\,M_{c^{\prime}})}.

The value of
\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)
does not depend on
T
, nor on the polarisation.

Proof.

Apply equation (
2.2
) and observations

(O1)
and
(O2)
of Section
2.1
.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

Let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is free over
R
and polarisable over a field
L
.
Then being
L
-linked is an
equivalence relation on the homogeneous components of
M
, and for
all
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in C
such that
M_{c_{1}}
,
M_{c_{2}}
, and
M_{c_{3}}
are
linked we have

\mathcal{C}_{c_{1},c_{2}}(M)=\mathcal{C}_{c_{2},c_{1}}(M)^{-1}\text{ and }\mathcal{C}_{c_{1},c_{3}}(M)=\mathcal{C}_{c_{1},c_{2}}(M)\mathcal{C}_{c_{2},c_{3}}(M).

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
M
be a polarisable graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is free over
R
,
and let
c,c^{\prime}\in C
be such that
M_{c}
and
M_{c^{\prime}}
are linked. Then we have

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)\in Q^{\times}/(R^{\times})^{2}.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.16
there exists
a polarisation on
M
that takes values in
Q
, and by Proposition

3.19
the value of
\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)
does not depend on
the polarisation.
∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is free over
R
.
Let
c
,
c^{\prime}\in C
, and set
c=c^{\prime}c^{-1}
. Suppose that there exists

T\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
such that
T\colon M\to M
is surjective. Then one has

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)\in R^{\times}/(R^{\times})^{2}
.

Proof.

By Nakayama’s lemma
[
46
, Theorem 2.4]
, the
\mathbf{T}
-module
endomorphism
T
of
M
, being surjective, is in fact an isomorphism.
This also implies that
\iota(T)
is an isomorphism.
Thus
\det(T\colon M_{c}\to M_{c^{\prime}})

and
\det(\iota(T)\colon M_{c^{\prime}}\to M_{c})
, evaluated with
respect to any
R
-bases on
M_{c}
and
M_{c^{\prime}}
, are units
in
R
, and the result follows.
∎

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
R\hookrightarrow\tilde{R}
be an embedding of commutative domains,
let
L
be a field containing
\tilde{R}
, and let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
that is free over
R
and that is polarisable over
L
.
Then for all
c
,
c^{\prime}\in C
one has

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)=\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(\tilde{R}\otimes_{R}M)\in L^{\times}/(\tilde{R}^{\times})^{2}.

Proof.

This is immediate from the definitions.
∎

The next result is not used in the sequel, but it illustrates a connection
between Hecke eigenvalues and regulator ratios. Proposition
J

is a special case.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
R=Q
be a field, let
\mathbf{T}_{1}
be a field, take
W
to be trivial,
let
M
be a graded
\mathbf{T}
-module.
Let
c\in C
have order
2
, assume that
M_{1}
and
M_{c}
are linked, by

T
, say, and let
T^{2}=a\in\mathbf{T}_{1}
.
Then
M
is polarisable,
and we have

\mathcal{C}_{1,c}(M)=\norm_{\mathbf{T}_{1}/Q}(a)^{n}\bmod{(Q^{\times})^{2}},

where
n
is the common dimension of
M_{1}
and
M_{c}
over
\mathbf{T}_{1}
, and
where
\norm_{\mathbf{T}_{1}/Q}
denotes the
Q
-algebra norm on
\mathbf{T}_{1}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.15
,
M
is polarisable.
Write the action of
T
on
M_{1}\oplus M_{c}
as a block-diagonal matrix
