\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\
B&0\end{pmatrix}
over the
field
\mathbf{T}_{1}
with
A,B
invertible. Then
T^{2}=a
acts as

\begin{pmatrix}AB&0\\
0&BA\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{n}&0\\
0&a\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
\iota
is an automorphism of
\mathbf{T}
that is trivial
on
Q
, we have
\det_{Q}(\iota(B))=\det_{Q}(B)
.
We get
\mathcal{C}_{1,c}(M)=\det_{Q}(A)/\det_{Q}(\iota(B))\equiv\det_{Q}(AB)\equiv\norm_{\mathbf{T}_{1}/Q}(a^{n})\bmod{(Q^{\times})^{2}}
.
This proves the result.
∎

The next three results show that in many situations
regulator constants of a module can be factored as products of
regulator constants of “simpler” modules.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
M
be a polarisable graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is free over
R
and
that decomposes as a direct sum
M=\bigoplus_{i}M_{i}
of polarisable graded

\mathbf{A}
-submodules. Let
c,c^{\prime}\in C
, and assume that
M_{c}
and
M_{c^{\prime}}

are linked.
Then for every
i
,
(M_{i})_{c}
and
(M_{i})_{c^{\prime}}
are
linked, and we have

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)=\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M_{i}).

Proof.

The first assertion is clear. Also, by hypothesis, we may choose a
polarisation on
M
that makes all summands
M_{i}
pairwise orthogonal, whence
the second assertion follows.
∎

Proposition 3.26
.

Let
R=Q
be a field, and let
M
be a polarisable graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
that is free over
R
.
Let
c,c^{\prime}\in C
, and assume that
M_{c}
and
M_{c^{\prime}}

are linked.
Let the image of
\mathbf{T}_{1}
in
\mathbf{A}
decompose as a direct product
\prod_{i}B_{i}

of
Q
-algebras with involution,
and for all
i
, let
M_{i}=B_{i}\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}M
. Then for all
i
, the graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
M_{i}
is polarisable,
(M_{i})_{c}

and
(M_{i})_{c^{\prime}}
are linked, and we have

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)=\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M_{i})\in Q^{\times}/(Q^{\times})^{2}.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.13
, since
M
is polarisable, the
M_{i}
are
polarisable. We have a decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{i}M_{i}
.
The last two assertions follow from Lemma
3.25
.
∎

Proposition 3.27
.

Let
R=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and let
M
be a polarisable graded
\mathbf{A}
-module that is free over
R
.
Let
\mathcal{N}=\{\mathfrak{n}\cap\iota(\mathfrak{n}):\mathfrak{n}\in\MaxSpec(\mathbf{T}_{1})\}
, and for all

\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
let
M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}=(\mathbf{T}_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}M
.
Then for all
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
, the graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
is
polarisable.

Let
c,c^{\prime}\in C
, and assume that
M_{c}
and
M_{c^{\prime}}

are linked.
Then for all
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
,
(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})_{c}

and
(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})_{c^{\prime}}
are linked, and we have

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)=\prod_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}/(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2}.

Proof.

By
[
23
, Corollary 7.6]
, there is a
decomposition
\mathbf{T}_{1}=\prod_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{T}_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}}
, with the product running over the
maximal ideals of
\mathbf{T}_{1}
. Accordingly, by grouping terms, there is a direct sum
decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}}M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
.
By Proposition
3.13
, since
M
is polarisable, the
M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
are
polarisable. The last two statements follow from Lemma
3.25
.
∎

4.
Vignéras pairs of manifolds

In the present section we will introduce the number theoretic situation
we are interested in, a general version of the Vignéras construction,
and apply the results of Section
3

to this situation, deriving inexplicit self-twist conditions for various
kinds of isospectralities.
We fix the following notation.

F

a number field.

\mathbb{Z}_{L}

the ring of integers of a number field or
p
-adic field
L
.

\mathfrak{p}

a maximal ideal of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
.

\mathbb{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}}

ring of integers of the completion
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

\mathfrak{N}

a non-zero ideal of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
.

\infty

the set of all real places of
F
.

\Cl_{F}(\mathfrak{N}\mathcal{V})

the ray class group with modulus
\mathfrak{N}
times a subset
\mathcal{V}

of
\infty

U_{F}(\mathfrak{N}\mathcal{V})

the group
\{u\in\mathbb{Z}_{F}^{\times}:u-1\in\mathfrak{N}\text{ and }v(u)>0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{V}\}
.

F_{\mathbb{R}}

\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}F
.

\mathbb{A}_{F,f}

the ring
(\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes F
of finite adèles of
F
.

\mathbb{A}_{F}

the ring
F_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{A}_{F,f}
of adèles of
F
.

D

a division algebra over
F
of degree
d\geq 2
that is not totally definite.

\mathbb{H}

the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra of Hamilton quaternions.

\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)
,

the sets of infinite places of
F
that are real and split in
D
,

\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{C}}(D)
,

respectively complex (and necessarily split in
D
),

\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)

respectively ramified in
D
.

\mathcal{O}

a maximal order in
D
.

\mathbb{G}

the algebraic group corresponding to
D^{\times}
, i.e.
representing

the functor
(\bullet\otimes_{F}D)^{\times}
on the category of
F
-algebras.

G_{\infty}

\mathbb{G}(F_{\mathbb{R}})
.

K_{\infty}

a maximal compact subgroup of
G_{\infty}
.

Z_{\infty}

the centre of
G_{\infty}
.

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
d_{\mathfrak{p}}

are defined up to isomorphism by

\mathbb{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{F}}\mathcal{O}\cong\Mat_{d_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})
, where

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a local ring.
From now on fix such isomorphisms.

\Delta_{\mathfrak{p}}

the division algebra
F_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

\delta_{D}

the finite product
\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{d-d_{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{F}
.

K(\mathfrak{p}^{i})
,

\GL_{d_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})
if
i=0
,

1+\Jac(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})^{i}\Mat_{d_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathbb{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}})
if
i>0
(i.e. the kernel of

\quad{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

reduction modulo
\Jac(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})^{i}
), where
\Jac(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})
is
the maximal ideal of
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

K(\mathfrak{N})

\prod_{\mathfrak{p}^{i}\parallel\mathfrak{N}}K(\mathfrak{p}^{i})
, the product running over all maximal ideals of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
.

K_{f}

an open subgroup of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})
containing

K(\mathfrak{N})
and

such that
K_{f}/\centre(K_{f})
is compact.

\nrd

the reduced norm map from
\mathbb{G}
to the multiplicative group
\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{+}

\{g=(g_{v})_{v}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}):\det(g_{v})>0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)\}
.

H^{+}

H\cap\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{+}
, where
H
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.

\mathcal{Y}

\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}K_{\infty}K_{f}
.

X

G_{\infty}/(Z_{\infty}K_{\infty})
.

C

\nrd(\mathbb{G}(F)^{+})\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/\nrd(K_{f})
.

\Gamma_{c},\,c\in C

\Gamma_{g}=\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\cap gK_{f}g^{-1}
where the class of
\nrd(g)
in
C
is
c
.

Y_{c}

\Gamma_{c}\backslash X
.

\normaliser(K_{f})

the normaliser of
K_{f}
in
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})
.

W

F^{\times}\backslash(Z_{\infty}K_{\infty}\times\normaliser(K_{f})/K_{f})
.

C_{\iso}

see discussion after Proposition
4.2
, in
particular Equation (
4.3
).

\bar{c}

the class of
c
in
C_{\iso}
, where
c\in C
.

c^{\perp}

the set of
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}
such that
\chi(c)=1
, where
c\in C
.

\mathbb{T}

the subalgebra of
\mathbb{Z}[K_{f}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})/K_{f}]

generated by the

K_{f}\mathbb{G}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})K_{f}
for all
\mathfrak{p}\nmid\delta_{D}\mathfrak{N}
.

We will now recall some basic facts about these objects. We refer to

[
27
, §2.6, §5.5, §15.2]
for these facts and further material.

The double quotient
\mathcal{Y}
is a compact orientable orbifold.
Fix a set
\mathcal{R}
of double coset
representatives of
\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})/K_{f}
. For each

g\in\mathcal{R}
, define
\Gamma_{g}=\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\cap gK_{f}g^{-1}
, and
Y_{g}=\Gamma_{g}\backslash X
.
Then each
Y_{g}
is connected, and one has
\mathcal{Y}=\bigsqcup_{g\in\mathcal{R}}Y_{g}
.
The reduced norm map induces a bijection between the component
set
\mathcal{R}
and the group
C
, and we will from now on implicitly use
this bijection, and will index the connected components of
\mathcal{Y}
by
C
.
The group
C
is a quotient of

\nrd(\mathbb{G}(F)^{+})\backslash\nrd(\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}))/\nrd(K(\mathfrak{N}))=\Cl_{F}(\mathfrak{N}\infty).

We have

G_{\infty}\cong\GL_{d}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)}\times\GL_{d}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{C}}(D)}\times\GL_{d/2}(\mathbb{H})^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)}.

The maximal compact subgroup
K_{\infty}
is accordingly a product of maximal
compact subgroups over the infinite places of
F
, is unique up to conjugation,
and may be taken to be

K_{\infty}\cong\O_{d}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)}\times\U_{d}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{C}}(D)}\times\U_{d/2}(\mathbb{H})^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)}.

Accordingly, the dimension of
X
(and therefore also of
\mathcal{Y}
) can be computed to be

\left(\frac{d^{2}+d-2}{2}\right)\cdot\#\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)+\left(d^{2}-1\right)\cdot\#\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{C}}(D)+\left(\frac{d^{2}-d-2}{2}\right)\cdot\#\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)
.
Note, in particular, that when
d=2
, the condition on
D
to be not totally definite,
equivalently the set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{C}}(D)
to be non-empty, is precisely
the condition to ensure that the dimension of
\mathcal{Y}
is positive.

We now give an alternative description of
\mathcal{Y}
, which we will use in the next
section.

Lemma 4.1
.

We have
Z_{\infty}^{+}K_{\infty}^{+}=G_{\infty}^{+}\cap(Z_{\infty}K_{\infty})
.

Proof.

One inclusion is obvious. For the other, it suffices to argue place by place, and the
statement is only non-empty at the places
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)
. Locally at such
a place, the claim is that we have
\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}\SO_{d}(\mathbb{R})=\GL_{d}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{\times}\O_{d}(\mathbb{R}))
,
where
\GL_{d}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
denotes the group of real
d\times d
matrices with positive determinant,
and where
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
denotes the subgroup of
\GL_{d}(\mathbb{R})
consisting of scalar matrices.
To prove the claim, suppose that we have
z\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
,
k\in\O_{d}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\det(zk)>0
. If we have
\det(z)>0
, then we also have
\det(k)>0
, hence

zk\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}\SO_{d}(\mathbb{R})
. If, on the other hand, we have
\det(z)<0
, so that in particular
d
is odd,
then
-1\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
has determinant
-1
, and is also an orthogonal matrix,
so that we have
zk=(-1\cdot z)(-1\cdot k)\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}\SO_{d}(\mathbb{R})
. This proves
the claim, and hence the lemma.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

We have canonical isomorphisms

\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{G}(F)\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}^{+}K_{\infty}^{+}K_{f}

and

C=F^{\times}_{+}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/\nrd(K_{f})=F^{\times}\backslash(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\times\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)})/\nrd(K_{f}),

where
F^{\times}_{+}=\{\alpha\in F:v(\alpha)>0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)\}
.

Proof.

First we prove the first isomorphism.
By Lemma
4.1
, the inclusion
G_{\infty}^{+}\hookrightarrow G_{\infty}
induces
an injection

\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash(G_{\infty}^{+}\cdot\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}))/Z_{\infty}^{+}K_{\infty}^{+}K_{f}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash(G_{\infty}\cdot\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}))/Z_{\infty}K_{\infty}K_{f}.

Since the map
\det\colon K_{\infty}\to\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)}
is surjective, this
injection is also a surjection.

Next, the same inclusion induces an injection

\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash(G_{\infty}^{+}\cdot\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}))/Z_{\infty}^{+}K_{\infty}^{+}K_{f}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}(F)\backslash(G_{\infty}\cdot\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}))/Z_{\infty}^{+}K_{\infty}^{+}K_{f}.

By weak approximation, the map

\sign\nrd\colon\mathbb{G}(F)\to\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)}
is also surjective, so
this injection, too, is a surjection, and the first isomorphism is proven.

Next, we show that
C=F^{\times}_{+}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/\nrd(K_{f})
. Indeed,
by the Hasse–Schilling–Maass Theorem
[
57
, Theorem 33.15]
,
the image of
\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}
under the reduced norm map is precisely
F^{\times}_{+}
.

The last isomorphism follows from the fact that the map

F^{\times}\to\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)}
is surjective.
∎

Applying Proposition
4.2
, we have a map

\displaystyle\nu\colon W

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle C=F^{\times}\backslash(\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(D)\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)}\times\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times})/\nrd(K_{f})

\displaystyle w=(w_{\infty},w_{f})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle F^{\times}(\sign\nrd(w_{\infty}),\nrd(w_{f}))^{-1}\nrd(K_{f}),

and we define, as in Section
3
, the quotient
C_{\iso}=C/\nu(W)
.
We have a canonical isomorphism

(4.3)

\displaystyle C_{\iso}\cong F^{\times}\backslash(\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)})\times\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/\nrd(\normaliser(K_{f})),

and since
\normaliser(K_{f})
contains
\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}K_{f}
, the group
C_{\iso}
is a quotient of

\nrd(\mathbb{G}(F)^{+})\backslash\nrd(\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}))/\nrd(K_{\infty}K(\mathfrak{N})\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times})\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt\Cl_{F}(\mathfrak{N}\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D))/(\Cl_{F}(\mathfrak{N}\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D)))^{d}.

Elements of
W
act via isometries on
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{G}(F)\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}^{+}K_{\infty}^{+}K_{f}
by right multiplication.

The algebra
\mathbb{T}
is commutative. It is generated by double cosets

T_{\mathcal{D}}=K_{f}\diag(\mathcal{D})K_{f},

where

\mathcal{D}=(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{a}_{d})\in\left(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}\right)^{d}

is such that for every
\mathfrak{p}|\delta_{D}\mathfrak{N}
and for every
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
one has
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=0
,
i.e.
\mathcal{D}
is a
d
-tuple of non-zero fractional ideals of
F
that are coprime
to
\delta_{D}\mathfrak{N}
.
The algebra
\mathbb{T}
is a subalgebra of
\mathbb{Z}[K_{f}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})/K_{f}]
.

There is an involution on
\mathbb{Z}[K_{f}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})/K_{f}]
– see
[
65
, Ch. 1, §8.6(d)]
–
given by inversion, which induces an involution
\iota
on
\mathbb{T}
. Explicitly, it is given by

\displaystyle\iota\colon\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{T},

\displaystyle T_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle T_{\mathcal{D}^{-1}}.

This involution is extended to
\mathbb{T}[W]
by defining it to be inversion on
W
.

The algebra
\mathbb{T}[W]
is graded by
C
,
the grading being defined on
\mathbb{T}
by the condition that
T_{\mathcal{D}}

for
\mathcal{D}
as above belongs to the homogeneous component of the image of

[\mathcal{D}]^{-1}=\prod_{i=1}^{d}[\mathfrak{a}_{i}]^{-1}\in\Cl_{F}(\mathfrak{N}\infty)

in the quotient
C
, and being induced on
W
by the homomorphism
\nu
.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
L
be a field.
A
Hecke eigensystem over
L
is an
L
-algebra homomorphism

a=(a_{\mathcal{D}})_{\mathcal{D}}\colon L\otimes\mathbb{T}\to L
,
1\otimes T_{\mathcal{D}}\mapsto a_{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
M
an
(L\otimes\mathbb{T})
-module.
The
multiplicity in
M
of a Hecke eigensystem
(a_{\mathcal{D}})

is the
L
-dimension of the space of all elements
f\in M

satisfying

T_{\mathcal{D}}f=a_{\mathcal{D}}f

for all
\mathcal{D}\in\left(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}\right)^{d}
.
A
Hecke eigensystem in
M
is a Hecke eigensystem that has multiplicity
at least
1
in
M
.

Given a Hecke eigensystem
(a_{\mathcal{D}})_{\mathcal{D}}
in
M
and
\chi\in\widehat{C}
,
we say that
(a_{\mathcal{D}})_{\mathcal{D}}

admits a self-twist
by
\chi
if for all

\mathcal{D}\in\left(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}\right)^{d}
satisfying

\chi([\mathcal{D}])\neq 1
one has
a_{\mathcal{D}}=0
. In the case when
R
contains all

n
-th roots of unity, where
n
is the order of
\chi
, we may view
\chi

as taking values in
R^{\times}
, and then the condition of admitting a self-twist
is equivalent to the condition that for all
\mathcal{D}
one has
\chi([\mathcal{D}])\cdot a_{\mathcal{D}}=a_{\mathcal{D}}
.

The following two lemmas will link the self-twist condition with Proposition
3.6
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
R
be a domain, let
\mathbf{T}=R\otimes\mathbb{T}
, let
M
be a
\mathbf{T}
-module that is finitely
generated over
R
, let
\mathfrak{p}\in\MaxSpec(R)
, and let
E
be a simple
\mathbf{T}
-submodule
of
R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{R}M
. Then there exists a finite field extension
S
of
R/\mathfrak{p}
and a
Hecke eigensystem
(a_{\mathcal{D}})_{\mathcal{D}}
over
S
in
S\otimes_{R}M
such that whenever

T_{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathbb{T}
annihilates
E
, one has
a_{\mathcal{D}}=0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the annihilator of
E
in
R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes\mathbf{T}
, and let

S=(R/\mathfrak{p}\otimes\mathbf{T})/\mathfrak{m}
be the residue field of
\mathfrak{m}
. Since
M
is finitely generated over
R
,
the field
S
is a finite extension of
R/\mathfrak{p}
. The module
S\otimes_{R/\mathfrak{p}}E

is a
1
-dimensional
S
-vector subspace of
S\otimes_{R}M
. For every
\mathcal{D}
,
define
a_{\mathcal{D}}
to be the image of
T_{\mathcal{D}}
in
S
. Then
(a_{\mathcal{D}})_{\mathcal{D}}
is a Hecke eigensystem
with the claimed property.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
M
be a graded
\mathbb{T}[W]
-module,
and let
c\in C
. Suppose that there exists a field
L
,
a character
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
, and a Hecke
eigensystem over
L
in
L\otimes M
admitting a self-twist by
\chi
. Then
no element of
\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
acts invertibly on
M
.

Proof.

Let
f\in L\otimes M
be an eigenvector whose eigensystem has a self-twist by
\chi
. We
will show that every
T\in\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
annihilates
f
. First, let
T=tw\in\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
,
where
w\in W
and
t\in\mathbb{T}_{c\nu(w)^{-1}}
. Since
\chi(c\nu(w)^{-1})=\chi(c)\neq 1
,
we have
tf=0
, and therefore
Tf=wtf=0
. Since
\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
is spanned by elements

T
of this form, the proof of the claim is complete.
Finally, if an element of
\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
acted invertibly on
M
, it would also
act invertibly on
L\otimes M
; this proves the lemma.
∎

Fix an integer
i\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
. As in the introduction,
let
\Delta
denote the Laplace operator. If
Y
is either
\mathcal{Y}
or

Y_{c}
for some
c\in C
, and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
we will consider the following
R
-modules
\mathcal{F}(Y)
attached to
Y
for
suitable rings
R
:

•

\mathcal{F}(Y)=\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=\lambda}(Y)
,
the space of real differential
i
-forms on
Y
on which
\Delta
acts
by multiplication by
\lambda
, with
R=\mathbb{R}
,

•

\mathcal{F}(Y)=\mathcal{H}^{i}(Y)=\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=0}
,
the space of real harmonic
i
-forms, with
R=\mathbb{R}
,

•

the homology group
\mathcal{F}(Y)=H_{i}(Y,R)
, where
R
is any domain, e.g.
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

For each of these collections
\mathcal{F}
of
R
-modules one has a direct sum decomposition

\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})=\bigoplus_{c\in C}\mathcal{F}(Y_{c}),

which defines a
C
-grading on
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})
. We will apply the formalism of Section
3

with
\mathbf{T}=R\otimes\mathbb{T}
. The Hecke algebra

\mathbf{T}[W]
naturally acts on
M=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})
,
and the image
\mathbf{A}
of
\mathbf{T}[W]
in
\End(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y}))

is an
R
-algebra that is finitely generated as an
R
-module.
The algebra
\mathbf{A}
inherits the
C
-grading and the involution
\iota
from
\mathbb{T}[W]
, and

\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})
is a graded
\mathbf{A}
-module in the sense of Section
3.3
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
c\in C
, and let
i\geq 0
be an integer.

(1)

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
exactly one of the following two statements is true:

(i)

there exist
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
and a Hecke
eigensystem over
\mathbb{C}
in
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=\lambda}(\mathcal{Y})
admitting a self-twist
by
\chi
;

(ii)

there exists
T\in\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
inducing, for all
b\in C
, an isomorphism
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
-modules

T\colon\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=\lambda}(Y_{b})\to\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=\lambda}(Y_{cb}).

(2)

At least one of the following two statements is true:

(i)

there exist
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
and a Hecke
eigensystem over
\mathbb{C}
in the module

\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=\lambda}(\mathcal{Y})

admitting a self-twist by
\chi
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
, the manifolds
Y_{b}
and
Y_{cb}
are

i
-isospectral.

(3)

Let
p
be a prime number. Then exactly one of the
following two statements is true:

(i)

there exist
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
and a Hecke
eigensystem over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\otimes H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})
admitting a self-twist by
\chi
;

(ii)

there exists
T\in\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
inducing, for all
b\in C
, an isomorphism
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
-modules

T\colon H_{i}(Y_{b},\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})\to H_{i}(Y_{cb},\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}).

(4)

Let
\mathfrak{n}
be a maximal ideal of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
,
let
p
be the characteristic of
\mathbb{T}_{1}/\mathfrak{n}
. Then exactly one of the
following two statements is true:

(i)

there exist
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
and a Hecke
eigensystem over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}

in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\otimes H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}}
admitting a self-twist by
\chi
;

(ii)

there exists
T\in\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
inducing, for all
b\in C
, an isomorphism
of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
-modules

T\colon H_{i}(Y_{b},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}}\to H_{i}(Y_{cb},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}}.

Proof.

(1)

Take
R=\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that there are no
\chi
and Hecke eigensystem
as in the statement. By Lemma
4.5
, the
graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
M=\Omega_{\Delta=\lambda}^{i}(\mathcal{Y})

satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
3.6
, so
there exists
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
that acts invertibly on
M
.
The image of
\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
in
\mathbf{A}
generates the real vector space
\mathbf{A}_{c}
,
and being an isomorphism is a non-empty Zariski-open condition,
so there also exists
T\in\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
realising an isomorphism as claimed.
The converse follows from Lemma
4.6
.

(2)

This follows immediately from
(1)
.

(3)

Take
R=\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
. Suppose, once again, that there are no

\chi
and Hecke eigensystem as in the statement.
By Lemma
4.5
, the
graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
M=H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
satisfies the hypotheses of
Proposition
3.6
, so
there exists
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
that acts invertibly on
M
.
Clearing denominators and lifting to
\mathbb{T}[W]
, we obtain a
T\in\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
as required.
The converse follows from Lemma
4.6
.

(4)

The proof is identical to that of the previous part,
with
R=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
M=H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathfrak{n}}
.∎

Remark 4.8
.

Notice that in part
(2)
there is no claim that the isospectrality
is realised by a Hecke operator, and we do not get an equivalence but only one implication.

The group
G_{\infty}
acts by unitary operators on the complex Hilbert space

\rL^{2}(\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}K_{f})

of square-integrable functions

\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}K_{f}\to\mathbb{C}
, and also, for each
c\in C
,
on the analogously defined space
\rL^{2}(\Gamma_{c}\backslash G_{\infty}/Z_{\infty})
.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
c
,
c^{\prime}\in C
. We say that the groups
\Gamma_{c}
and
\Gamma_{c^{\prime}}

are
representation equivalent
if there is an isomorphism of unitary

G_{\infty}
-representations

\rL^{2}(\Gamma_{c}\backslash G_{\infty}/Z_{\infty})\cong\rL^{2}(\Gamma_{c^{\prime}}\backslash G_{\infty}/Z_{\infty}).

The Hecke algebra
\mathbb{T}[W]
naturally acts on
\rL^{2}(\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}K_{f})
,
making the
\rL^{2}
-space a
C
-graded
\mathbb{T}[W]
-module.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
c\in C
. Then
at least one of the following two statements is true:

(i)

there exist
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
and a Hecke
eigensystem over
\mathbb{C}
in the module
\rL^{2}(\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}K_{f})

admitting a self-twist by
\chi
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
the groups
\Gamma_{b}
and
\Gamma_{cb}
are representation equivalent.

Proof.

Suppose that there are no
\chi
and Hecke eigensystem as in the statement.
Fix
b\in C
.
By
[
25
]
(see also
[
28
]
), there are decompositions
into Hilbert orthogonal direct sums of isotypical unitary
representations of
G_{\infty}
with finite multiplicities

\rL^{2}(\mathbb{G}(F)^{+}\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}K_{f})\cong\widehat{\bigoplus}_{\Pi}V(\Pi),

and, for all
c^{\prime}\in C
,

\rL^{2}(\Gamma_{c^{\prime}}\backslash G_{\infty}/Z_{\infty})\cong\widehat{\bigoplus}_{\Pi}V(\Pi)_{c^{\prime}},

both sums indexed by pair-wise non-isomorphic irreducible unitary
representations
\Pi
of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.
The action of
\normaliser(K_{f})/K_{f}\times Z_{\infty}
commutes with that
of
G_{\infty}
, and
K_{\infty}
is contained in
G_{\infty}
, so
each
V(\Pi)
is also a
\mathbb{T}[W]
-module, and is
graded, with
c^{\prime}
-component
V(\Pi)_{c^{\prime}}
.

Let
\Pi
be an irreducible unitary representation of
G_{\infty}
.
We claim that
\Pi
occurs with the same multiplicities in
V(\Pi)_{b}

and in
V(\Pi)_{cb}
.
We have a Hilbert direct sum decomposition of isotypical unitary
finite-dimensional representations of
K_{\infty}

V(\Pi)|_{K_{\infty}}\cong\widehat{\bigoplus}_{\sigma}V(\Pi)(\sigma),

with the sum running over pair-wise non-isomorphic irreducible unitary representations
\sigma

of
K_{\infty}
, again compatible with the grading, and where each summand is
preserved by
\mathbb{T}[W]
. Let
\sigma
be an
irreducible unitary representation of
K_{\infty}
such that
V(\Pi)(\sigma)\neq 0
, so that it is enough to prove that
\dim V(\Pi)(\sigma)_{b}=\dim V(\Pi)(\sigma)_{cb}
. Let
R=\mathbb{C}
and let
\mathbf{A}
be the image
of
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{T}[W]
in the endomorphism algebra of the
finite-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
V(\Pi)(\sigma)
, so that
\mathbf{A}
is a
finite-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-algebra. By Lemma
4.5

the graded
\mathbf{A}
-module
M=V(\Pi)(\sigma)

satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
3.6
, so there
exists
T\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
that acts invertibly on
M
, therefore realising an
isomorphism

T\colon V(\Pi)(\sigma)_{b}\to V(\Pi)(\sigma)_{cb}.

This proves the desired equality of dimensions, and therefore the desired
equality of multiplicities for
\Pi
.
Since
\Pi
was arbitrary, we obtain an isomorphism as claimed.
∎

For the rest of the section, fix a degree
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and a prime number
p
,
and let
\mathbf{A}
be the image of
\mathbb{T}[W]

in
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
, so that
H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
is a graded

\mathbf{A}
-module. We will apply the results of Section
3
with

R=\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbf{T}=\mathbb{T}
.
For every
c\in C
there is a canonical positive definite pairing on
\mathcal{H}^{i}(Y_{c})

[
4
, Notation 3.2 and Lemma 3.3]
,
[
58
]
.
By the Hodge and de Rham
theorems, this harmonic forms pairing induces a non-degenerate
\mathbb{R}
-valued pairing
on
H_{i}(Y_{c},\mathbb{R})
. The
i
-th regulator
\Reg_{i}(Y_{c})
of
Y_{c}
is defined to be
the covolume of the lattice
H_{i}(Y_{c},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
in
H_{i}(Y_{c},\mathbb{R})
.

Definition 4.11
.

Define
\mathcal{N}=\{\mathfrak{n}\cap\iota(\mathfrak{n}):\mathfrak{n}\in\MaxSpec(\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{1})\}
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Define a pairing on
M=H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
induced by the harmonic forms pairing
on the summands
H_{i}(Y_{c},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
for
c\in C
and by making the distinct
summands pairwise orthogonal. Then this defines a polarisation on

M
in the sense of Definition
3.10
.
Moreover, for every
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
the graded
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes\mathbf{A}
-module
M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}

is polarisable.

Proof.

The adjoint of each
T\in\mathbb{T}
with respect to the harmonic forms pairing is
\iota(T)
,
and
W
acts by isometries (see also Remark

3.11
), so the pairing defines a polarisation on the graded module
\mathcal{H}^{i}(\mathcal{Y})
.
Moreover, the Hodge–de Rham isomorphism between
\mathcal{H}^{i}(\mathcal{Y})
and
H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{R})
is

\mathbf{A}
-equivariant, so the harmonic forms pairing is an
\mathbb{R}
-valued polarisation
on the
\mathbf{A}
-module
M
. The last assertion follows from Proposition
3.27
.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
c
,
c^{\prime}\in C
, let
M=H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
,
and suppose that
M_{c}
and
M_{c^{\prime}}

are linked in the sense of Definition
3.1
. Then:

(1)

we have

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)=\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{c})^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(Y_{c^{\prime}})^{2}},

where recall that the invariant
\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)
was defined in Definition
3.18
;

(2)

we have

\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{c})^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(Y_{c^{\prime}})^{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times};

(3)

for all
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
, the homogeneous components
(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})_{c}
and
(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})_{c^{\prime}}
are linked,
and we have

\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)\equiv\prod_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\mod{(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2}},

where
\mathcal{C}_{c,c^{\prime}}(M)\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
is viewed as an element of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
.

Proof.

(1)

By definition,
\Reg_{i}(Y_{c})^{2}
is the determinant of the Gram matrix of the
harmonic forms pairing with respect to any
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
H_{i}(Y_{c},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
, and
similarly for
Y_{c^{\prime}}
. The claim therefore follows from Lemma
4.12

and Proposition
3.19
.

(2)

The assertion follows from combining part
(1)
and Corollary
3.21
.

(3)

The assertion follows from Lemma
3.23
,
Lemma
4.12
, and Proposition
3.27
.∎

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
c\in C
.
Then exactly one of the following two statements is true:

(i)

there exist
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
and a Hecke
eigensystem over
\mathbb{C}

in
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}^{i}(\mathcal{Y})
admitting a self-twist by
\chi
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
the homogeneous components
H_{i}(Y_{b},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
and
H_{i}(Y_{cb},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}

are linked.

Proof.

Suppose that there are no
\chi
and Hecke eigensystem
as in the statement. The isomorphism between
\mathcal{H}^{i}(\mathcal{Y})
and
H^{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{R})
is

\mathbf{A}
-equivariant. Lemma
4.5
, applied
to
M=H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
, and Corollary
3.7

imply that
M_{c}
and
M_{c^{\prime}}
are linked.
The converse follows from Lemma
4.6
.
∎

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
c\in C
, and let
p
be a prime number, let
M=H_{i}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{Z})_{\free}
, and let
\mathcal{N}
be as
in Definition
4.11
.

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
.
Then at least one of the following two statements is true:

(i)

there exist
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}

and a Hecke eigensystem over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\otimes(M/\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}M)
admitting a self-twist by
\chi
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
, the homogeneous components
(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})_{b}
and
(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})_{cb}
are linked, and we have

\mathcal{C}_{b,cb}(M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times};

(2)

At least one of the following two statements is true:

(i)

there exist
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
and a Hecke
eigensystem over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\otimes M
admitting a self-twist by
\chi
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
, the homogeneous components
M_{b}
and
M_{cb}
are linked,
and we have

\frac{\Reg_{i}(Y_{b})^{2}}{\Reg_{i}(Y_{cb})^{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}.

Proof.

(1)

Suppose that there are no
\chi
and Hecke eigensystem
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\otimes(M/\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}M)
as in the statement.
By Lemma
4.5
the assumptions of Proposition

3.6
are satisfied for
M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
,
so there exists
T\in\mathbf{A}_{c}
that acts invertibly on
M_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
.
The result follows from Lemma
3.22
.

(2)

Suppose that there are no
\chi
and Hecke eigensystem
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\otimes M
as in the statement, and let
b\in C
.
By the same argument as in part
(1)
, the homogeneous components

M_{b}
and
M_{cb}
are linked.
The claimed equality then follows by combining
Lemma
4.13
and part
(1)

of the present theorem.∎

5.
Self-twists in characteristic
0
and automorphic induction

Assumption 5.1
.

We keep the notation of Section
4
, but we
assume that
d=2
, i.e. that
D
is a quaternion algebra.
In particular we have
Z_{\infty}=Z_{\infty}^{+}
.

In this section we prove Theorem
D

for representation equivalence,
\Omega^{\bullet}
-,
\Omega^{0}
-, and
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-isospectrality,
Theorem
G
parts
(1)
and
(2)
,
and Theorem
C
. To prove Theorem
D

we will use the results of Section
4
and make the self-twist conditions
in those results explicit. To that end, we will relate the various
\mathbb{T}
-modules
to automorphic representations, directly or using Matsushima’s formula. By
an automorphic induction theorem of Langlands, the self-twist condition yields the
existence of Hecke characters of quadratic extensions of
F
with specific properties.
To make these properties explicit, we inspect closely irreducible representations
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and how they interact with automorphic induction,
Jacquet–Langlands transfer, and Matsushima’s formula.
Theorem
G
parts
(1)

and
(2)
constitute a refinement of Theorem

D
for
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-isospectrality.
To deduce Theorem
C
,
we count the Hecke characters that contribute to the various Casimir eigenspaces.
This is achieved by proving local bounds, Lemmas
5.2
and
5.5
,
and then counting points in lattices.

The content of Sections
5.1
–
5.3
is well-known.

•

In Section
5.1
we fix our notation and recall basic facts about Hecke characters.

•

In Section
5.2.1
we recall the classification of irreducible representations
of
\GL_{2}(E)
for an Archimedean local field
E
, including their
K
-type,
Casimir eigenvalue, and central character, and how they arise from automorphic induction and
Jacquet–Langlands transfer.

•

In Section
5.2.2
we recall the classification of smooth irreducible representations
of
\GL_{2}(E)
for a non-Archimedean local field
E
, and we state which ones arise
as automorphic inductions, as well as some properties of conductors.

•

In Section
5.3
we recall the results on automorphic representations of
\GL_{2}

that we need, namely strong multiplicity
1
, automorphic induction, Jacquet–Langlands correspondence,
and Matsushima’s formula.

General references for these facts are
[
26
, Appendix B]

[
27
]
,
[
33
]
.

In Section
5.4
we prove Theorem
D
for the isospectralities
listed above and Theorem
G
parts
(1)
and
(2)
.
That section is divided into subsections, one for each type of isospectrality.
Finally, in Section
5.5
we prove Theorem
C
.

Throughout Section
5
all group representations will be on complex Hilbert spaces.

5.1.
Automorphic representations of
\GL_{1}

In this section, we fix some notation for Hecke characters. For a general
reference, see e.g.
[
38
, Ch. XIV]
.

Let
G
be a locally compact group. Recall that a
quasi-character
of
G
is a
continuous homomorphism
G\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and a
character
of
G
is a
quasi-character that is unitary.

Every quasi-character of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
is of the form

\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k,s)\colon x\mapsto\sign(x)^{k}|x|^{s}

for a unique pair
(k,s)\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}
.

Every quasi-character of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is of the form

\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k,s)\colon z\mapsto\left(\frac{z}{|z|}\right)^{k}|z|^{2s}

for a unique pair
(k,s)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}
.

In both cases, such a quasi-character is unitary if and only if one has
s\in i\mathbb{R}
.

If
E
is a non-Achimedean local field,
\mathfrak{p}
is the maximal ideal of the ring
of integers
\mathbb{Z}_{E}
of
E
, and
\Psi
is a quasi-character of
E^{\times}
, then
there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
\Psi
is trivial on

(1+\mathfrak{p}^{m})\cap\mathbb{Z}_{E}^{\times}
.
The
conductor
of
\Psi
is
\mathfrak{p}^{m}
, where
m
is the smallest such integer.

A
Hecke quasi-character
of
F
is a quasi-character of
F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}
, and a
Hecke character
of
F
is a character of
F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}
.

Every Hecke quasi-character
\Psi
of
F
is of the form

\Psi=\prod_{v}\Psi_{v}

for quasi-characters
\Psi_{v}
of
F_{v}^{\times}
, where
v
runs over the places
of
F
. For every infinite place
v
, we define
k_{v}
and
s_{v}
by
writing
\Psi_{v}=\Psi_{F_{v}}(k_{v},s_{v})
. Note that this definition depends on
identifications between
F_{v}
and
\mathbb{C}
for every complex place
v
.
Replacing a chosen identification with its complex conjugate does not
change
s_{v}
but negates
k_{v}
. Everything that we write in this section
is insensitive to this ambiguity, as long as we impose the following convention:
whenever
L/F
is a quadratic extension of number fields,
v
is a complex place of
F
,
and
w
,
w^{\prime}
are places of
L
extending
v
, we always choose isomorphisms

L_{w}\cong\mathbb{C}
and
L_{w^{\prime}}\cong\mathbb{C}
that extend
the same
arbitrarily chosen
isomorphism
F_{v}\cong\mathbb{C}
.

A Hecke quasi-character
\Psi
is called
algebraic
if for every real
place
v
we have
s_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}
and for every complex place
v
we have
s_{v}+k_{v}/2\in\mathbb{Z}
.
If
\Psi
is an algebraic Hecke character, then for every
embedding
\tau\colon F\to\mathbb{C}
there exists a uniquely determined
q_{\tau}\in\mathbb{Z}

such that for all
\alpha\in F^{\times}
that are positive at all real places we have

\prod_{v|\infty}\Psi_{v}(\alpha)=\prod_{\tau\colon F\to\mathbb{C}}\tau(\alpha)^{q_{\tau}},

and the products run over all infinite places
v
, respectively embeddings
\tau\colon F\to\mathbb{C}
.
We refer to the collection
(q_{\tau})_{\tau\in\Hom(F,\mathbb{C})}
as the
type
of
an algebraic Hecke character.

5.2.
Representations of
\GL_{2}
over local fields

Throughout this subsection, let
E
be a local field. We will fix the required
notation and briefly recall the relevant facts for irreducible representations of
\GL_{2}(E)
.
General references for this subsection are
[
26
, Appendix B]
and

[
33
, §5-6, 14-15]
.

Let
L
be a quadratic étale
E
-algebra, i.e. either a quadratic field
extension of
E
or a direct product
E\times E
. There is an
automorphic
induction
functor
\AI_{E}^{L}
from the category of irreducible representations
of
\GL_{1}(L)
to that of irreducible representations of
\GL_{2}(E)
. If
\sigma

is the non-trivial
E
-linear automorphism of
L
, then for all characters

\Psi
of
L^{\times}
we have
\AI_{E}^{L}(\Psi^{\sigma})=\AI_{E}^{L}(\Psi)
.

Let
A
be a quaternion algebra over
E
. The local Jacquet–Langlands correspondence
[
33
]

attaches to every irreducible representation
\Pi
of
A^{\times}
a
representation
\JL_{A}(\Pi)
of
\GL_{2}(E)
, well-defined up to isomorphism,
sometimes referred to as the
Jacquet–Langlands transfer
of
\Pi
from

A^{\times}
to
\GL_{2}
.
If
A
is split, then
\JL_{A}(\Pi)
is isomorphic to
\Pi
. We will
say more about the properties of
\JL_{A}(\Pi)
in some special cases below.

5.2.1.
Archimedean fields

In this subsection, assume that
E
is Archimedean.

Given an irreducible representation
\Pi
of
\GL_{2}(E)
on a vector
space
V
, its
central character
is the group homomorphism

\zeta_{\Pi}\colon E^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
characterised by the property
that for all
x\in E^{\times}\subset\GL_{2}(E)

and all
v\in V
one has
\Pi(x)(v)=\zeta_{\Pi}(x)v
.

Let
K
be a compact subgroup of
\GL_{2}(E)
. The restriction of every representation of
\GL_{2}(E)
to
K

decomposes as a Hilbert direct sum of irreducible representations.
Fix a full set of representatives
u
of isomorphism classes of
irreducible representations of
K
. The
K
-type
of a
representation
\Pi
of
\GL_{2}(E)
is the multiset of representatives
u

with multiplicities
\dim\Hom_{K}(u,\Pi)
.

Given an irreducible representation
\Pi
of
\GL_{2}(E)
or of
\mathbb{H}^{\times}
, the

Casimir operator
, which we take to be the negation of
[
9
, Chapters 1 and 2]
(see
also
[
10
, Theorem 2.2.1]
in the case of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
), acts as a
scalar on a dense subspace of the underlying
vector space. We call this scalar the
Casimir eigenvalue
of
\Pi
.
The definition of the Casimir operator depends on a choice of scalar
multiple of the Killing form, and we choose the normalisation for which,
in the case of
\GL_{2}(E)
, the quotient by a maximal compact subgroup has
constant curvature
-1
, and in the case of
\mathbb{H}^{\times}
, the form
agrees with that for
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
after complexification.

Isomorphism classes of irreducible representations of
\SO_{2}(\mathbb{R})
have
dimension
1
and are parametrised
by integers, with
k\in\mathbb{Z}
corresponding to

r(k)\colon\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\mapsto e^{ik\theta}.

Isomorphism classes of irreducible representations of
\SU_{2}(\mathbb{C})
are
parametrised by non-negative integers, with
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
corresponding to
the representation

s(k)=\Sym^{k}\mathbb{C}^{2},

which has dimension
k+1
, where
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is equipped with the standard action
of
\SU_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Isomorphism classes of irreducible representations of
\mathbb{H}^{\times}
are parametrised by
elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\times\mathbb{C}
, with
(k,\mu)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\times\mathbb{C}

corresponding to the representation

t(k,\mu)=\Sym^{k-2}\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\nrd^{\mu/2},

which has dimension
k-1
, where
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is equipped with the action
of
\mathbb{H}^{\times}
induced by the map
\mathbb{H}^{\times}\to\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
defined
by
h\mapsto\nrd(h)^{-1/2}j(h)
for some isomorphism
j\colon\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\cong\Mat_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
The eigenvalue of the Casimir operator on
t(k,\mu)

is
\frac{k}{2}(1-\frac{k}{2})
and the central character of
t(k,\mu)

is
\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k,\mu)
.

Every irreducible representation of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is isomorphic to one
from exactly one of the following families.

•

Irreducible principal series
\PS_{\mathbb{R}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})
, where
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}

are quasi-characters
\Psi_{i}=\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k_{i},s_{i})
such that
s=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}-s_{2}+1)
and
k=k_{1}-k_{2}

satisfy
2s\notin k+2\mathbb{Z}
.

•

Discrete series and limits of discrete series
\DS(k,\mu)
, where
k\geq 1
is an integer and
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
.

•

Finite-dimensional
representations
\FD_{\mathbb{R}}(k,\Psi)=\Sym^{k-2}\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(\Psi\circ\det)
, where
k\geq 2
is an
integer and
\Psi
is a quasi-character of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.

Every irreducible representation of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is isomorphic to one
from exactly one of the following families.

•

Irreducible principal series
\PS_{\mathbb{C}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})
, where
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}

\Psi_{i}=\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k_{i},s_{i})

and

.

•

Finite-dimensional representations
\FD_{\mathbb{C}}(k,k^{\prime},\Psi)=\Sym^{k-2}\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\Sym^{k^{\prime}-2}\bar{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes(\Psi\circ\det)
, where
k,k^{\prime}\geq 2

are integers,
\Psi

\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and
\mathbb{C}^{2}
denotes the standard representation
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, while
\bar{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
denotes its composition with complex conjugation.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional representation of
K_{\infty}
. Then
there exists
\kappa_{\infty}>0
such that for every irreducible
representation
\Pi_{\infty}
of
G_{\infty}
one has

\dim\Hom_{K_{\infty}}(V,\Pi_{\infty})\leq\kappa_{\infty}.

Proof.

The assertion follows by inspection of the above lists of the
irreducible representations of
\GL_{2}(E)
for
E=\mathbb{R}
and
E=\mathbb{C}
.
∎

The automorphic inductions
\AI_{E}^{L}
from
\GL_{1}(L)
to
\GL_{2}(E)
are as follows.

•

Extension
\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
:
\AI_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k,s))=\DS(1+|k|,2s)
.

•

Extension
\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
:
let
\Psi_{i}=\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k_{i},s_{i})
for
i=1,2
be ordered such
that
\vRe(s_{1})\geq\vRe(s_{2})
. Then:

–

\AI_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}(\Psi_{2},\Psi_{1})=\AI_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})=\PS_{\mathbb{R}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})
if
s_{1}-s_{2}+1\notin k_{1}+k_{2}+2\mathbb{Z}
;

–

\AI_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}(\Psi_{2},\Psi_{1})=\AI_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})=\FD_{\mathbb{R}}(k,\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k_{2},s_{2}+\frac{1}{2}))
, where
k=s_{1}-s_{2}+1
, if
k\in k_{1}+k_{2}+2\mathbb{Z}
and
k\neq 1
;

–

\AI_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}(\Psi_{2},\Psi_{1})=\AI_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})=\DS(1,2s_{1})=\DS(1,2s_{2})
if
s_{1}=s_{2}
and
k_{1}\not\equiv k_{2}\mod{2}
.

•

Extension
\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
:
let
\Psi_{i}=\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k_{i},s_{i})
for
i=1,2
be ordered such that
\vRe(s_{1})\geq\vRe(s_{2})
,

s=s_{1}-s_{2}
, and
k=k_{1}-k_{2}
. Then:

–

\AI_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}(\Psi_{2},\Psi_{1})=\AI_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})=\PS_{\mathbb{C}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})

if
2s\notin k+2\mathbb{Z}
or
|s|<\frac{|k|}{2}+1
;

–

\AI_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}(\Psi_{2},\Psi_{1})=\AI_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})=\FD_{\mathbb{C}}(s+\frac{k}{2}+1,s-\frac{k}{2}+1,\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k_{2},s_{2}+\frac{1}{2}))
otherwise.

The Jacquet–Langlands transfer
\JL_{\mathbb{H}}
from
\mathbb{H}^{\times}
to
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is

\JL_{\mathbb{H}}(t(k,\mu))=\DS(k,\mu)\text{ for }(k,\mu)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\times\mathbb{C}.

We now record the isomorphism classes of some representations of various compact groups.

Let
\mathfrak{so}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
be the Lie algebras of
\SO_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, respectively of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
both equipped with the adjoint action of
\SO_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
We have the following isomorphisms of
\SO_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-representations.

2

Now, let
\mathfrak{su}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be the Lie algebras of
\SU_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, respectively of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
both equipped with the adjoint action of
\SU_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
We have the following isomorphisms of
\SU_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representations.

i

5.2.2.
Non-Archimedean fields

An additional general reference for this subsection is
[
11
]
, particularly Chapters 8 and 13.

Let
E
be a
p
-adic field, let
\mathfrak{p}
be its maximal ideal.
Let
A
be the unique quaternion division algebra over
E
.
A representation of
A^{\times}
or of
\GL_{2}(E)
is called
smooth
if
every vector in the underlying vector space is fixed by some compact open subgroup.
We will assume that all our representations are smooth without repeating it.

Every irreducible representation of
A^{\times}
is finite-dimensional.
Every irreducible representation of
\GL_{2}(E)
is of exactly one of the
following types:

•

finite-dimensional representations: they are all of the form
g\mapsto\phi\circ\det
for a quasi-character
\phi\colon E^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,

•

irreducible principal series
\PS_{E}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})
,
where
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}\colon E^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
are quasi-characters such
that
\Psi_{1}\Psi_{2}^{-1}\notin\{|\cdot|^{\pm 1}\}
,

•

special representations,

•

supercuspidal representations.

The automorphic induction
\AI_{E}^{L}
from
\GL_{1}(L)
to
\GL_{2}(E)
satisfies the following.

•

An automorphic induction is never a special representation.

•

Extension
E\subset E\times E
: let
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}\colon E^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be quasi-characters.

–

\AI_{E}^{E\times E}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})=\PS_{E}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})

if
\Psi_{1}\Psi_{2}^{-1}\notin\{|\cdot|^{\pm 1}\}
.

–

\AI_{E}^{E\times E}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})=\phi\circ\det
for some
quasi-character
\phi\colon E^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
otherwise.

•

Quadratic field extension
E\subset L
: let
\sigma\in\Gal(L/E)
be the
non-trivial element, let
\chi\colon\Gal(L/E)\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the
non-trivial quadratic character, and let
\Psi\colon L^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}

be a quasi-character.

–

\AI_{E}^{L}(\Psi)
is supercuspidal if
\Psi\neq\Psi^{\sigma}
.

–

\AI_{E}^{L}(\Psi)=\PS_{E}(\phi,\phi\chi)
for some
quasi-character
\phi\colon E^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
otherwise.

For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let

K_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{r})=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right):c\equiv 0\bmod\mathfrak{p}^{r}\right\}\subset\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{E})

and

K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}^{r})=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right):d\equiv 1\bmod\mathfrak{p}^{r},c\equiv 0\bmod\mathfrak{p}^{r}\right\}\subset K_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{r}).

If
\Pi
is an irreducible representation of
\GL_{2}(E)
,
then its
conductor
is
\mathfrak{p}^{n}
where
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
is minimal
subject to the condition
\Pi^{K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}^{n})}\neq 0
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\Pi
be an irreducible representation, let
\mathfrak{p}^{n}
be its conductor, and let
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}
.
Then the space
\Pi^{K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i})}
of fixed points has dimension

i-n+1
.

Proof.

See
[
17
]
, see also
[
59
, Theorem 1.2.1 (ii)]
.
∎

In
A^{\times}
, we define
K_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{0})
to be the unit group of the unique maximal
order in
A^{\times}
. When we use the notation
K_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{0})
, it will be clear
from the context whether it refers to the subgroup of
A^{\times}

or to
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{E})
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
L/E
be an étale quadratic extension, let
\delta
be its discriminant,
let
\Psi\colon L^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a quasi-character such that

\AI_{E}^{L}(\Psi)
is infinite-dimensional, and let
\mathfrak{F}
be the conductor
of
\Psi
. Then the conductor of
\AI_{E}^{L}(\Psi)
is
\delta\cdot\norm_{L/E}(\mathfrak{F})
.

Proof.

See
[
59
, Table at the end of §1]
.
∎

The Jacquet–Langlands transfer
\JL_{A}
from
A^{\times}
to
\GL_{2}(E)
has the following property:
an irreducible representation
\Pi
of
\GL_{2}(E)
is in the image of
\JL_{A}
if and only if
\Pi

is special or supercuspidal.

Lemma 5.5
.

There exists
\kappa_{f}>0
such that for every irreducible
representation
\Pi_{f}
of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})
we have

\dim\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}\leq\kappa_{f}.

Proof.

Recall that we have
K(\mathfrak{N})\subset K_{f}
, so that for every irreducible
representation
\Pi_{f}
of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})
we
have
\dim\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}\leq\dim\Pi_{f}^{K(\mathfrak{N})}
. Write
K(\mathfrak{N})=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}K(\mathfrak{p}^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}})
and
\Pi_{f}=\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}
, where
\otimes^{\prime}
denotes a restricted tensor product.
Note that for every prime
\mathfrak{p}
we have
\dim\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{K(\mathfrak{p}^{0})}\leq 1
, and we
have
\dim\Pi_{f}^{K(\mathfrak{N})}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\dim\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{K(\mathfrak{p}^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}})}
.
Let
\mathfrak{p}
be such that
m_{\mathfrak{p}}>0
.

•

If
\mathfrak{p}
is split in
D
, then by Lemma
5.3
we have

\dim\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{K(\mathfrak{p}^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}})}\leq\dim\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}^{2m_{\mathfrak{p}}})}\leq 2m_{\mathfrak{p}}+1
, which depends only
on
K_{f}
.

•

If
\mathfrak{p}
is ramified in
D
, then consider the finite group
G=D_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}/(F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}K(\mathfrak{p}^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}}))
, and let
d(G)
be the maximal
dimension of an irreducible representation of
G
.
Since
\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}
is irreducible,
F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}
acts via a
character
\zeta
on
\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Therefore
\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{K(\mathfrak{p}^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}})}\zeta^{-1}
is an irreducible
representation of
G
, hence of dimension at most
d(G)
, which also
depends only on
K_{f}
.

This proves the existence of
\kappa_{f}
.
∎

5.3.
Automorphic representations of
\GL_{2}

For the duration of the subsection, let
\mathbf{G}
be one of the algebraic groups
\mathbb{G}
or
\GL_{2}
over
F
.

For a general definition of an automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
and of cuspidality
see
[
27
, Definitions 6.3.5 and 6.5.1]
. For our purposes, simpler definitions in special
cases will be sufficient.

A
discrete automorphic representation
of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is an irreducible subrepresentation of

\rL^{2}(\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/\mathbb{R}_{>0})
, where

\mathbb{R}_{>0}\subset\centre(\mathbf{G}(F_{\mathbb{R}}))=\prod_{v|\infty}\centre(\mathbf{G}(F_{v}))
is embedded diagonally.
For the relation with automorphic representations in the usual sense
see
[
27
, Theorem 6.6.4]
. Every automorphic representation of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is discrete
and cuspidal, and every cuspidal automorphic representation of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
can be identified
with a discrete automorphic representation, see
[
27
, Theorem 6.5.3]
.

Let
\Pi
be a discrete automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. Then
\Pi
can be
written as a restricted tensor product
\Pi_{\infty}\otimes\Pi_{f}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{v}\Pi_{v}

over the places
v
of
F
, where
\Pi_{\infty}
is a representation of
\mathbf{G}(F_{\mathbb{R}})
,

\Pi_{f}
is a representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})
, and for each place
v
, the
factor
\Pi_{v}
is a representation of
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
. The fixed point space

\Big(\mathop{\bigotimes\nolimits^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\delta_{D}\mathfrak{N}}\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}\Big)^{K_{f}}=\bigotimes_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\delta_{D}\mathfrak{N}}\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}^{0})}

is
1
-dimensional, and admits an action of
\mathbb{T}
, thus giving rise to a Hecke eigensystem.

If
\Pi
is a discrete automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
and
\chi

is a Hecke character of
F
, we write
\Pi\otimes\chi
as shorthand for the automorphic
representation
\Pi\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(\chi\circ\nrd)
.

Theorem 5.6

(Strong multiplicity
1
)
.

Every cuspidal automorphic representation occurs with multiplicity
1
in

\rL^{2}(\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/\mathbb{R}_{>0})
. Moreover,
if
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\prime}
are two cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathbf{G}

such that
\Pi_{v}
is isomorphic to
\Pi_{v}^{\prime}
for all but finitely
many places
v
of
F
, then
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\prime}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

See
[
33
]
,
[
3
, Theorem 5.1 (b), (c)]
, see also
[
27
, Theorems 11.4.3 and 11.7.2]
.
∎

Theorem 5.7

(Automorphic induction)
.

Let
L/F
be a quadratic extension of number fields and let
\sigma
be the
generator of its Galois group, and let
\chi
be the quadratic Hecke character
corresponding to the extension
L/F
by class field theory.
Let
\Psi
be a Hecke character of
L
.
The automorphic induction
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\AI_{F_{v}}^{F_{v}\otimes_{F}L}(\Psi_{v})
is an automorphic
representation of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.
The representation
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
is cuspidal if and only
if
\Psi^{\sigma}\neq\Psi
.
A cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is the automorphic
induction of a Hecke character of
L
if and only if
\Pi\otimes\chi\cong\Pi
.

Proof.

See
[
39
]
, see also
[
27
, Theorem 13.4.2]
.
∎

Theorem 5.8

(Jacquet–Langlands correspondence)
.

There exists a unique injection
\JL_{D}
from the class of isomorphism classes of
automorphic representations of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
to the class of isomorphism classes
of discrete automorphic representations of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
satisfying
\JL_{D_{v}}(\Pi_{v})=\JL_{D}(\Pi)_{v}
for
all places
v
.
The representation
\JL_{D}(\Pi)
is cuspidal if and only if
\Pi
is infinite-dimensional.
When restricting to the class of infinite-dimensional representations,
the image is the class of cuspidal automorphic representations
\Pi

such that for all places
v
, the local representation
\Pi_{v}
is in the
image of
\JL_{D_{v}}
.

Proof.

See
[
33
]
, see also
[
27
, Theorem 19.4.3]
.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Keep the notation as in Theorem
5.7
.
Let
\Pi
be an automorphic representation of
\mathbb{G}
such
that
\Pi\otimes\chi\cong\Pi
.
Then
\Pi
is infinite-dimensional, and there exists a Hecke character
\Psi
of
L
such that
\JL_{D}(\Pi)=\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
.

Proof.

If
\Pi
is finite-dimensional, then it is
1
-dimensional, and the hypothesis is never satisfied,
therefore
\Pi
is infinite-dimensional.
By Multiplicity
1
, Theorem
5.6
, the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence, being local-global
compatible, is also compatible with twisting. Applying Theorems
5.7

and
5.8
therefore gives the statement.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{k}
denote the Lie algebras of
G_{\infty}
and
K_{\infty}
,
respectively, both equipped with the adjoint action of
K_{\infty}^{+}
.

Theorem 5.10

(Matsushima’s formula)
.

We have

\Omega^{i}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\bigoplus_{\Pi}\Hom_{K_{\infty}^{+}}(\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda^{i}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k}),\Pi_{\infty})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}},

where the sum ranges over automorphic representations
\Pi
of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})

such that
\Pi_{\infty}
is trivial on
Z_{\infty}
.
Moreover, the isomorphism is equivariant with respect to:

•

the Laplace operator on the left hand side and the Casimir operator
acting on
\Pi_{\infty}
on the right hand side, and

•

the action of Hecke operators, acting on
\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}
on the right
hand side.

Proof.

This is a well-known adélic reformulation of the classical Matsushima formula
[
47
]
, see
[
8
, §1.2]
.
The equivariance with respect to Hecke operators follows from the canonicity
of the correspondence in ibid.
Note that the statement simplifies in our situation due to Multiplicity
1
, Theorem
5.6
.
∎

Definition 5.11
.

Given a collection
\underline{\bm{i}}=(i_{v})_{v}
of non-negative integers indexed
by the infinite places
v
of
F
, we define

\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{\Pi}\Hom_{K_{\infty}^{+}}\Big(\bigotimes_{v}\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda^{i_{v}}(\mathfrak{g}_{v}/\mathfrak{k}_{v}),\Pi_{\infty}\Big)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}.

Moreover, given a collection
\underline{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{v})_{v}
or real numbers
indexed by the infinite places
v
of
F
, define

\Omega_{\Delta=\underline{\bm{\lambda}}}^{\underline{\bm{i}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}\subset\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}

to be the subspace consisting of Laplace eigenvectors
on which the Laplace operator at each infinite place
v
has
eigenvalue
\lambda_{v}
. Let

\mathcal{H}^{\underline{\bm{i}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}=\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}_{\Delta=\underline{\bm{0}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}
be the space
of harmonic forms in
\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\underline{\bm{0}}
denotes the
zero vector.

Corollary 5.12
.

For every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=\lambda}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{\underline{\bm{\lambda}}}\bigoplus_{\underline{\bm{i}}}\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}_{\Delta=\underline{\bm{\lambda}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{H}^{i}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{\underline{\bm{i}}}\mathcal{H}^{\underline{\bm{i}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}},

where the sums run over all collections
\underline{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{v})_{v}
satisfying
\sum_{v}\lambda_{v}=\lambda
, respectively
all collections
\underline{\bm{i}}=(i_{v})_{v}
satisfying
\sum_{v}i_{v}=i
.

5.4.
Self-twist conditions

In this subsection we prove Theorem
D
for
representation equivalence,
\Omega^{\bullet}
-,
\Omega^{0}
-, and

\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
-isospectrality, and
Theorem
G
parts
(1)

and
(2)
.

5.4.1.
Representation equivalence

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\chi
be an order
2
Hecke character, let
L/F
be the corresponding
quadratic extension, and let
\sigma
denote the non-trivial
automorphism of
L/F
. Let
V
be a
K_{\infty}^{+}
-representation.
Then every Hecke eigensystem over
\mathbb{C}
in the
module
M=\Hom_{K_{\infty}^{+}}(V,\rL^{2}(\mathbb{G}(F)\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}K_{f}))
that admits a self-twist by
\chi
is attached to some automorphic
representation
\Pi=\Pi_{\infty}\otimes\Pi_{f}
of

\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
such that
\JL_{D}(\Pi)=\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
for a
unitary Hecke character
\Psi
of
L
satisfying all of the following:

(1)

for every place
v
of
F
that ramifies in
D
, there is a single
place
w
of
L
above
v
, and we have
\Psi_{w}^{\sigma}\neq\Psi_{w}
;

(2)

the parameters
(k_{w},s_{w})
of the
character
\Psi
satisfy the following conditions:

•

for every real place
v
of
F
that extends to a complex place
w

of
L
, we have
s_{w}=0
and
k_{w}
is odd;

•

for every real place
v
of
F
that extends to two real places
w,w^{\prime}

of
L
, we have
s_{w}+s_{w^{\prime}}=0
and
k_{w}+k_{w^{\prime}}=0\mod{2}
;

•

for every complex place
v
of
F
that extends to two complex
places
w,w^{\prime}
of
L
, we have
s_{w}+s_{w^{\prime}}=0
and
k_{w}+k_{w^{\prime}}=0
.

Each such Hecke eigensystem has multiplicity
\dim\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}\cdot\dim\Hom_{K_{\infty}^{+}}(V,\Pi_{\infty})
(which may be
0
).

Proof.

Hecke eigensystems over
\mathbb{C}
in
M
are attached to automorphic representations
\Pi=\Pi_{\infty}\otimes\Pi_{f}
of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
such that the central character
of
\Pi_{\infty}
vanishes on
Z_{\infty}
, and each such eigensystem has
multiplicity
\dim\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}\cdot\dim\Hom_{K_{\infty}^{+}}(V,\Pi_{\infty})
.
Hecke eigensystems over
\mathbb{C}

in
\rL^{2}(\mathbb{G}(F)\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/Z_{\infty}K_{f})
that admit a self-twist
by
\chi
are exactly those attached to automorphic representations
\Pi=\Pi_{\infty}\otimes\Pi_{f}
of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
such that
\Pi\otimes\chi\cong\Pi

and the central character of
\Pi_{\infty}
vanishes on
Z_{\infty}
.
By Corollary
5.9
, such
\Pi
are exactly the ones
satisfying

•

\Pi
is infinite-dimensional;

•

\JL_{D}(\Pi)=\AI_{L/F}(\Psi)
for some Hecke character
\Psi
of
L
;

•

the central character of
\Pi_{\infty}
vanishes on
Z_{\infty}
.

By Theorem
5.8
and Theorem
5.7
, the first two conditions are
equivalent to the existence of a Hecke character
\Psi
of
L
such that we have

\JL_{D}(\Pi)=\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
and
\Psi^{\sigma}\neq\Psi
.
Let
\Psi
be a Hecke character of
L
such that
\Psi^{\sigma}\neq\Psi
.
By Theorem
5.8
there exists
\Pi
such
that
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)=\JL_{D}(\Pi)
if and only if
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
is discrete
series at the infinite places that are ramified in
D
,
and special or supercuspidal at the finite places that are ramified in
D
.
Let
v
be a place of
F
that ramifies in
D
.
If
v
is an infinite place: by Subsection
5.2.1
,
the local component
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)_{v}
is a discrete series representation if
and only if
v
extends to a complex place
w
in
L
and
k_{w}\neq 0
,
equivalently there is a single place
w
of
L
above
v

and
\Psi_{w}^{\sigma}\neq\Psi_{w}
.
If
v
is a finite place: by Subsection
5.2.2
, the
local component
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)_{v}
is special or supercuspidal if and only
there is a single place
w
of
L
above
v

and
\Psi_{w}^{\sigma}\neq\Psi_{w}
. This proves that condition
(1)

of the conclusion of the proposition holds.
By Subsection
5.2.1
, the central character of
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)

vanishing on
Z_{\infty}
is equivalent to
condition
(2)
in the conclusion.
Since
\Psi
is trivial on
L^{\times}
, all the
s_{w}
have the same real part.
Therefore condition
(2)
implies that this real part is
0
,
so that
\Psi
is unitary.
∎

Definition 5.14
.

If
L/F
is a quadratic extension, then an
\rL^{2}
-shady character
of
L

is a unitary Hecke character
\Psi
of
L
such that
L
and
\Psi
have all of the following properties:

•

the field
L
and the character
\Psi
satisfy the conditions
(1)

and
(2)
in Proposition
5.13
, so that
in particular, by Theorems
5.7
and
5.8
,
there exists a unique automorphic representation
\Pi=\Pi_{\infty}\otimes\Pi_{f}

of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
satisfying
\JL_{D}(\Pi)=\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
;

•

we have
\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}\neq 0
.

The following is Theorem
D
for representation equivalence.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
c\in C
. Then at least one of the following statements is true:

(i)

there exist a character
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
,
with corresponding quadratic extension
L/F
, and an
\rL^{2}
-shady character of
L
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
the groups
\Gamma_{b}

and
\Gamma_{cb}
are representation equivalent.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.10
and
Proposition
5.13
.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

In Proposition
5.13
, condition
(1)
at a real
place
v
of
F
is equivalent to the following: if
v
ramifies in
D
,
then
v
extends to a complex place
w
of
L
and we have
k_{w}\neq 0
.

It will sometimes be useful to reformulate the conditions of
Proposition
5.13
as follows.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
L/F
be a quadratic extension, let
\sigma
denote the non-trivial
automorphism of
L/F
, and let
\Psi
be a Hecke character of
L
.
Then
\Psi^{\sigma}\Psi
has finite order if and only if all of the following
conditions hold:

•

for every real place
v
of
F
that extends to a complex place
w

of
L
, we have
s_{w}=0
;

•

for every real place
v
of
F
that extends to two real places
w,w^{\prime}

of
L
, we have
s_{w}+s_{w^{\prime}}=0
;

•

for every complex place
v
of
F
that extends to two complex
places
w,w^{\prime}
of
L
, we have
s_{w}+s_{w^{\prime}}=0
and
k_{w}+k_{w^{\prime}}=0
.

Proof.

At a place of
F
that extends to two places of
L
, the action of
\sigma

on
\Psi
swaps the parameters
(k,s)
. At a real place
v
of
F
that
extends to a complex place
w
of
L
, the action of
\sigma
on
\Psi

negates
k_{w}
and leaves
s_{w}
unchanged. Finally, a Hecke character of
L

has finite order if and only if all its
s
parameters are
0
and all
its
k
parameters at complex places are
0
. Putting these together gives the
lemma.
∎

Remark 5.18
.

When at least one real place of
F
ramifies in
L
, the Hecke characters
appearing in Theorem
5.15
are
partially algebraic Hecke
characters
in the sense of
[
48
, Section 5.5]
. In fact, they must
come from the construction given in the proof of
ibid.
, Proposition 41.

By omitting conditions from Theorem
5.15
, we recover previously known results.

Corollary 5.19
.

Let
c\in C
. Then at least one of the following statements is true:

(i)

there exists a character
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}
, with
corresponding quadratic extension
L/F
,
such that

(a)

\chi(c)=-1
, i.e.
\chi\not\in c^{\perp}
, and

(b)

for every place
v
of
F
that ramifies in
D
, there is a single
place of
L
above
v
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
the groups
\Gamma_{b}
and
\Gamma_{cb}
are
representation equivalent.

Remark 5.20
.

Corollary
5.19
seems much weaker than
Theorem
5.15
, but is sufficient to recover previously known
results.
It would be interesting to know how much weaker it actually is, specifically
whether, given
L/F
as in Corollary
5.19
,
there always exists a
\Psi
as in Theorem
5.15
.

Corollary 5.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{N}
be a non-zero ideal of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
coprime to
\delta_{D}
, and let
K_{f}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}^{i}\parallel\mathfrak{N}}K_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{i})
.
Let
c\in C
. Then at least one of the following statements is true:

(i)

we have that

(a)

D
is unramified at all finite places of
F
, and

(b)

there exists a quadratic extension
L/F
that is
ramified at exactly the same set of real places of
F

as
D
, such that all primes of
F

dividing
\mathfrak{N}
with odd exponent are split in
L
,
and such that all primes of
F

whose class in
C_{\iso}
is
\bar{c}
are inert in
L
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
the groups
\Gamma_{b}
and
\Gamma_{cb}

are representation equivalent.

Proof.

By Equation (
4.3
) and a local computation
[
67
, eqns. (23.2.4) and (23.2.8), Proposition 23.4.14]
,
we have
C_{\iso}=\Cl_{F}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{H}}(D))/\langle\mathfrak{a}^{2},\mathfrak{p}\text{ ramified in }D,\mathfrak{p}^{e}\|\mathfrak{N}\rangle
.
Assume that for some
b\in C
the groups
\Gamma_{b}
and
\Gamma_{cb}
are not
representation equivalent.
Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a finite place of
F
that ramifies in
D
. Then the class of
\mathfrak{p}
is
trivial in
C_{\iso}
, so
\mathfrak{p}
splits in
L
; but this
contradicts Corollary
5.19

(i)(b)
. Therefore, no
finite place ramifies in
D
.
Let
\chi
and
L/F
be as in Corollary
5.19
. By the
expression of
C_{\iso}
as a class group,
L/F
is unramified at all finite
places of
F
, split at all prime ideals dividing
\mathfrak{N}
with odd exponent, and the
set of real places of
F
that ramify in
L
is a subset of those that ramify
in
D
.
Corollary
5.19

(i)(b)
gives the other inclusion
between those sets of ramified real places.
Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a finite place of
F
whose class in
C_{\iso}
equals
\bar{c}
.
Then
\chi(\mathfrak{p})\neq 1
by Corollary
5.19

(i)(a)
,
so that
\mathfrak{p}
is inert in
L
.
∎

Remark 5.22
.

The groups of the form
\Gamma_{c}
for
c\in C
are exactly the ones of the form
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
,
where
\mathcal{O}
is an Eichler order of level
\mathfrak{N}
.
Corollary
5.21
is essentially the criterion used
in
[
44
]
to investigate isospectrality of a large number of
pairs of hyperbolic orbifolds of dimension
2
and
3
,
using the selectivity method
[
44
, Theorem 2.17 and Theorem 2.19]
.

Corollary 5.23
.

Assume that there is a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{F}
that is ramified in
D
, and that
there exists an element
g\in\normaliser(K_{f})
such that the valuation
\ord_{\mathfrak{q}}(\nrd(g))
is
even for every finite place
\mathfrak{q}\neq\mathfrak{p}
and such that
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\nrd(g))
is odd.
Then for all
c,c^{\prime}\in C
the groups
\Gamma_{c}

and
\Gamma_{c^{\prime}}
are representation equivalent.

Proof.

Let
c
,
c^{\prime}\in C
and assume that
\Gamma_{c}
and
\Gamma_{c^{\prime}}
are not
representation equivalent, and let
L/F
be as in
Corollary
5.19
.
By definition of
C_{\iso}
, the class of
\nrd(g)
in
C_{\iso}
is
trivial. On the other hand, since
C_{\iso}
has exponent dividing
2
, the
valuation assumptions imply that the class of
\nrd(g)
equals the class
of
\mathfrak{p}
. This implies that
\mathfrak{p}
splits in
L
, contradicting
Corollary
5.19

(i)(b)
.
Therefore, the groups
\Gamma_{c}

and
\Gamma_{c^{\prime}}
are representation equivalent.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

Corollary
5.23
is essentially the same criterion as
in
[
55
, Section 3]
: the condition (H3) in that paper (with
v_{0}=\mathfrak{p}
) implies
that we can take
g
to be a uniformiser of
D_{\mathfrak{p}}
at
v_{0}
and
g_{v}=1

for
v\neq v_{0}
. One superficial difference is that the representation equivalent groups
in
[
55
]
are arithmetic subgroups of
\SL_{1}(D)
, whereas ours are
subgroups of
\GL_{1}(D)
; by choosing appropriate levels
K_{f}
, our method can be adapted to
the
\SL_{1}(D)
setting, but we chose to stick to
\GL_{1}(D)
for simplicity of
the exposition. The similarity is not surprising: our Hecke eigenvalue systems
with a self-twist exactly correspond to the endoscopic representations studied
in
[
36
]
and playing an important role in
[
55
]
. However,
our method proves representation equivalence
without an analysis of the Labesse–Langlands multiplicity formula.

5.4.2.
Differential forms

Proposition 5.25
.

Let
\chi
be an order
2
Hecke character, with corresponding
quadratic extension
L/F
, let
\sigma
denote the non-trivial
automorphism of
L/F
, and let
\underline{\bm{i}}=(i_{v})_{v}
and
\underline{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{v})_{v}

be as in Definition
5.11
. Then the Hecke eigensystems over
\mathbb{C}

in
\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}_{\Delta=\underline{\bm{\lambda}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}
that admit a self-twist
by
\chi
are the Hecke eigenvalue systems attached to an automorphic representation
\Pi=\Pi_{\infty}\otimes\Pi_{f}

of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
such that
\JL_{D}(\Pi)=\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
for some unitary Hecke character
\Psi
of
L
satisfying
all of the following conditions:

(1)

for every place
v
of
F
that ramifies in
D
, there
is a single place
w
of
L
above
v
, and we have
\Psi_{w}^{\sigma}\neq\Psi_{w}
;

(2)

for every real place
v
of
F
that extends to a complex place
w
of
L

we have

•

i_{v}=0
if
v
is ramified in
D
;

•

i_{v}=1
otherwise;

\lambda_{v}=0
, and
\Psi_{w}=\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k,0)
with
k\in\{\pm 1\}
;

(3)

for every real place of
F
that extends to two real places
w
,

w^{\prime}
of
L
we have
i_{v}\in\{0,1,2\}
,
\Psi_{w}=\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k,s)
and

\Psi_{w^{\prime}}=\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k^{\prime},s^{\prime})
where
k\equiv k^{\prime}\mod 2
, and
s=it=-s^{\prime}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}

satisfying
\tfrac{1}{4}+t^{2}=\lambda_{v}
;

(4)

for every complex place
v
of
F
, extending to places
w
and
w^{\prime}
of
L
,
we have
\Psi_{w}=\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k,s)
and
\Psi_{w^{\prime}}=\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k^{\prime},s^{\prime})
where either

•

i_{v}\in\{0,1,2,3\}
,
k=k^{\prime}=0
, and
s=it=-s^{\prime}
with
t\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
1+4t^{2}=\lambda_{v}
; or

•

i_{v}\in\{1,2\}
,
k=-k^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}
, and
s=it=-s^{\prime}
with
t\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
4t^{2}=\lambda_{v}
;

each such Hecke eigenvalue system occurring with multiplicity
2^{r}\dim\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}
,
where
r
is the number of real places
v
of
F
for which one has
i_{v}=1
.

Proof.

We apply Proposition
5.13
with
V=\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}_{\Delta=\underline{\bm{\lambda}}}(\mathcal{Y})_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Let
\Psi
and
\Pi
be as in Proposition
5.13
.
For every infinite place
w
of
L
, write
s_{w}=it_{w}
with
t_{w}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Write
K_{\infty}^{+}=\prod_{v}K_{v}
, with the product running over the infinite places of
F
,
and for each such place
v
, let
H_{v}=\Hom_{K_{v}}(\Lambda^{i_{v}}(\mathfrak{g}_{v}/\mathfrak{k}_{v}),\Pi_{v})
.

Let
v
be a real place of
F
that extends to a complex place
w
of
L
.
Then we have
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)_{v}=\DS(1+|k_{w}|,0)
. By the local computation in
Section
5.2.1
the space
H_{v}
is non-trivial if and only if
either

•

the place
v
is unramified in
D
,
i_{v}=1
,
k_{w}\in\{\pm 1\}
, and
\lambda_{v}=0
,
in which case
\dim H_{v}=2
; or

•

the place
v
is ramified in
D
,
i_{v}=0
,
k_{w}\in\{\pm 1\}
, and
\lambda_{v}=0
,
in which case
\dim H_{v}=1
.

Let
v
be a real place of
F
that extends to two real places
w
,
w^{\prime}
of
L
.
Then we necessarily have
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)_{v}=\PS(\Psi_{w},\Psi_{w^{\prime}})
. Moreover,
the space
H_{v}
is non-trivial if and only if
i_{v}\in\{0,1,2\}
and
\lambda_{v}=\tfrac{1}{4}+t_{w}^{2}
.
In this case,
\dim H_{v}=2
if
i_{v}=1
, and
\dim H_{v}=1
otherwise.

Let
v
be a complex place of
F
, extending to two places
w
and
w^{\prime}
of
L
.
Then we necessarily have
\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)_{v}=\PS(\Psi_{w},\Psi_{w^{\prime}})
. Moreover,
the space
H_{v}
is non-trivial if and only if one of the following holds:

•

k_{w}=0
,
i_{v}\in\{0,1,2,3\}
,
\lambda_{v}=1+4t_{w}^{2}
;

•

k_{w}\in\{\pm 1\}
,
i_{v}\in\{1,2\}
,
\lambda_{v}=4t_{w}^{2}
.

When
H_{v}
is non-trivial, we have
\dim H_{v}=1
.
Taking the tensor product of the spaces
H_{v}
over all places
v\mid\infty
gives the result.
∎

Definition 5.26
.

If
L/F
is a quadratic extension, and
\underline{\bm{i}}
and
\underline{\bm{\lambda}}
are as in
Proposition
5.25
, then an
(\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}_{\Delta=\underline{\bm{\lambda}}})
-shady character

of
L
is a unitary Hecke character
\Psi
of
L
such that
L
and
\Psi
have
all of the following properties:

•

the field
L
and the character
\Psi
satisfy the conditions
(1)
–
(4)

in Proposition
5.25
, so that
in particular, by Theorems
5.7
and
5.8
,
there exists a unique automorphic representation
\Pi=\Pi_{\infty}\otimes\Pi_{f}

of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
satisfying
\JL_{D}(\Pi)=\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
;

•

we have
\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}\neq 0
.

We now prove a general version of Theorem
D
, which we
will then specialise.

Theorem 5.27
.

Let
c\in C
, let
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and let

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Then exactly one of the following statements is true:

(i)

there exist a character
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
, with
corresponding quadratic extension
L/F
,
collections
\underline{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{v})_{v}
and
\underline{\bm{i}}=(i_{v})_{v}
of real numbers,
respectively non-negative integers, indexed by the infinite places
v
of
F
,
such that
\sum_{v}\lambda_{v}=\lambda
and
\sum_{v}i_{v}=i
,
and an
(\Omega^{\underline{\bm{i}}}_{\Delta=\underline{\bm{\lambda}}})
-shady character of
L
;

(ii)

there exists
T\in\mathbb{T}[W]_{c}
inducing, for all
b\in C
, an
isomorphism of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
-modules

T\colon\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=\lambda}(Y_{b})\to\Omega^{i}_{\Delta=\lambda}(Y_{cb}).

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.7

(1)
,
Corollary
5.12
, and Proposition
5.25
.
∎

Definition 5.28
.

If
L/F
is a quadratic extension, with non-trivial automorphism
\sigma
,
then an
\Omega^{\bullet}
-shady character
of
L
is a unitary Hecke
character
\Psi
of
L
such that
L
and
\Psi
have all of the following properties:

(1)

for every place
v
of
F
that ramifies in
D
, there
is a single place
w
of
L
above
v
, and we have
\Psi_{w}^{\sigma}\neq\Psi_{w}
;

(2)

for every real place
v
of
F
that
extends to a complex place
w
of
L
we have
\Psi_{w}=\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k,0)

with
k\in\{\pm 1\}
;

(3)

for every real place of
F
that extends to two real places
w
,

w^{\prime}
of
L
we have
\Psi_{w}=\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k,s)
and

\Psi_{w^{\prime}}=\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k^{\prime},s^{\prime})
where
k\equiv k^{\prime}\mod 2
and
s=-s^{\prime}\in i\mathbb{R}
;

(4)

for every complex place
v
of
F
, extending to places
w
and
w^{\prime}
of
L
,
we have
\Psi_{w}=\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k,s)
and
\Psi_{w^{\prime}}=\Psi_{\mathbb{C}}(k^{\prime},s^{\prime})
where

k=-k^{\prime}\in\{-1,0,1\}
and
s=-s^{\prime}\in i\mathbb{R}
;

(5)

the unique automorphic representation
\Pi
of
\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
such
that
\JL_{D}(\Pi)=\AI_{F}^{L}(\Psi)
satisfies
\Pi_{f}^{K_{f}}\neq 0
.

The following is Theorem
D

for
\Omega^{\bullet}
-isospectrality.

Corollary 5.29
.

Let
c\in C
. Then at least one of the following statements is true:

(i)

there exist a character
\chi\in\widehat{C}_{\iso}\setminus c^{\perp}
,
with corresponding quadratic extension
L/F
with non-trivial
automorphism
\sigma
, and an
\Omega^{\bullet}
-shady character of
L
;

(ii)

for all
b\in C
the manifolds

Y_{b}
and
Y_{cb}
are
i
-isospectral for all
i\geq 0
.

Proof.

Suppose that part
(i)
does not hold. Then nor
does part
(i)
of Theorem
5.27
for any
i
and
\lambda
.
Thus part
(ii)
of Theorem
5.27
holds
for every
i
and
\lambda
, whence we deduce that part
(ii)

of the corollary holds.
∎

Definition 5.30
.

If
L/F
is a quadratic extension, with non-trivial automorphism

,

of

\Psi
of

and

of

that ramifies in

, there

of

above

, and we have
\Psi_{w}^{\sigma}\neq\Psi_{w}

of

, the algebra

;

(3)

that extends to two real places

,

of

and

\Psi_{w^{\prime}}=\Psi_{\mathbb{R}}(k^{\prime},s^{\prime})

(5)

the unique automorphic representation

The following is Theorem

for

Corollary 5.31
.

Let

. Then at least one of the following statements is true:

, with

,
